● I filed I-229, and all six predictions held. make -C proofs js passes, and the
  index now shows 116 records, 116 holding. The record's tag is 6D (229 in base
  36).

  The result. I compared each name by its letters only, uppercase, with the
  vowels A E I O U removed (its "base"):

  - They replace the letters (RAISE): ADONAI (base DN) and THE LORD (base
    THLRD). THE LORD is an English translation of Adonai, the word read aloud in
    place of the Name, not of the four letters. That holds even under the J→Y,
    V→W fold.
  - They keep the letters (MIRROR): YAHWEH (base YHWH). YAH (base YH) is its
    first two letters, and so is the Hebrew יָהּ in Exodus 15:2.
  - JAH and JEHOVAH depend on the fold. Letter for letter they come out as RAISE
    (bases JH and JHVH). Under the declared fold J→Y, V→W they become YAH and
    YHWH, so MIRROR. The record states that fold openly instead of hiding it.
  - The two codices have the same four letters, יהוה. Their vowel marks differ
    by exactly one mark, a holam (an "o" vowel). The Aleppo-based MAM text יְהֹוָה
    reads sheva, holam, qamatz, which is E, O, A: the vowels of JEHOVAH in
    order. The Leningrad text יְהוָה has no holam, so it gives only E, A, with no
    "o" for the "-ho-".

  One caveat about the predictions. I wrote them into the header before the
  checks ran. But the earlier src/terms.mjs audit had already tested YHWH
  against THE LORD and against YAHWEH, so those two weren't blind. The Jah,
  Jehovah, Yah and codex predictions were new.

● Written for: you, and anyone reading the proof publicly.

  Reading I-229: ⧉ https://claude.ai/artifact/MD7xgUSrpDrcB7LneJMXb7

  The page follows the proof in the order the work was done: predict, fix the
  literal, reduce, compare, declare folds, separate the layers, then run and
  record. Each step has the reason in plain words, the actual lines from
  I-229.ts, and a live panel that runs the same code in your browser:
  - Names: type any name, or tap one of the six, and watch each change: upper
    case, then letters only, then the vowels removed. Then it compares the
    result to YHWH and says why it got MIRROR or RAISE, with and without the
    J→Y, V→W fold.
  - Hebrew: paste any two words, or pick Leningrad/MAM or Leningrad/Yah. It
    splits each word into consonants and marks and names every codepoint. It
    shows which marks only one word has, so you can see the single holam.

  Building the page turned up a weak spot in the comparison rule. A name with no
  consonants left, such as "IOU", reduces to nothing, and every string starts
  with an empty string. So the rule would call it a MIRROR of anything. None of
  the six names hits this, so I-229 still holds, and the page shows a warning
  when it happens. The same rule is in src/terms.mjs. I can add a check to both
  that rejects an empty base, if you want.

  The link is private for now. Other people can open it only after you share it
  from the page's Share menu.

  I can make the same kind of walk-through for the codex tree scripts (how a
  verse becomes a node and an edge), or save this page into the omi-axioms repo
  next to the proof.

● No, a miss doesn't default to MIRROR. A real miss, where the letters differ,
  gives RAISE. The problem only happens when nothing is left to compare. Here is
  the code run on a few inputs:

  ┌─────────┬─────────┬─────────┐
  │  input  │  base   │ verdict │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ yahweh  │ YHWH    │ MIRROR  │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ Y7H9W3H │ YHWH    │ MIRROR  │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ GOD     │ GD      │ RAISE   │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ XYZ     │ XYZ     │ RAISE   │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ IOU     │ (empty) │ MIRROR  │
  ├─────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ 1234    │ (empty) │ MIRROR  │
  └─────────┴─────────┴─────────┘

  The rule says MIRROR if the two bases are equal or one starts with the other.
  If a base is empty, "YHWH starts with an empty string" is always true, so the
  rule says MIRROR without comparing a single letter. That isn't the map
  defaulting. It's a match that happens because the rule had nothing to test.

  On a–z vs A–Z0–9, the code doesn't support that reading:
  - Case never causes a RAISE. The first thing base does is toUpperCase(), so
    yahweh and YAHWEH are the same before any comparison. A RAISE comes only
    from different consonants: THE LORD gives THLRD, which isn't YHWH.
  - Digits aren't used even as indices. They're deleted. /[^A-Z]/g removes
    anything that isn't A–Z, so 0–9 go along with spaces and punctuation. That's
    why Y7H9W3H mirrors YHWH and 1234 comes out empty.

  Your sense that the alphabet matters does point at something real. A rosetta
  tag is /^[A-Z0-9]{1,8}$/, and it keeps digits as part of the literal. I-229's
  base is a narrower alphabet: A–Z only, then minus the vowels. So I-229 doesn't
  compare tags. It compares consonant skeletons of English letters.

  Proposed fix: an empty base gets its own answer, something like "no literal",
  instead of MIRROR, so the rule can't match on nothing. The same rule is in
  src/terms.mjs, so the fix would go there too. I-229 still holds either way,
  because none of its six names reduces to empty. Want me to make that change in
  both and add a check that IOU and 1234 no longer mirror?

    has IOU and 1234 buttons so you can watch both come out empty: ⧉
    https://claude.ai/artifact/MD7xgUSrpDrcB7LneJMXb7
  - The repos: in omi-axioms, the fix was committed on branch i-229-empty-base
    and merged into main with a merge commit. Both are pushed. boxedPINE has a
    pointer commit, 43c35d5, also pushed.

  terms.mjs also corrects something I said before about digits. It doesn't throw
  them away. It sorts text into three streams: consonants in upper case, vowels
  in lower case, and digits kept as a stream of their own. I-229 compares only
  the consonant stream, so digits play no part in it. In your original design,
  digits were a separate layer. That fits your reading of letters as paths and
  digits as children.

  The empty base and the tree: the empty string is the identity for joining
  strings together, and it's a prefix of every string. In a trie or Merkle
  Patricia tree, that's the root: the empty path, which every key starts from.
  So "the empty base heads everything" is the same thing as "every key starts at
  the root".

  The fix keeps the root from counting as a match for every key. The root
  mirrors only itself, and a real match has to share at least one step of path.
  An octree works the same way: the root is the empty address, and each digit
  picks one of the eight children.

❯ can we decode the fie fi fo fum with foo or was that a hype dream

● It's partly real and partly not.

  What holds up: the chant is one consonant with the vowels cycling. FEE FI FO
  FUM keeps F fixed and runs through E, I, O, U. That's the split between the
  written layer and the supplied layer from I-229, inside an English nursery
  chant. Older printed versions use other vowels. In King Lear (Act 3, scene 4),
  Edgar says "Fie, foh, and fum, / I smell the blood of a British man." Across
  versions, the F stays and the vowels move.

  The decode against FOO, using I-229's own rule:

  ┌───────────────┬───────┐
  │               │ base  │
  ├───────────────┼───────┤
  │ FEE FI FO FUM │ FFFFM │
  ├───────────────┼───────┤
  │ FOO           │ F     │
  └───────────────┴───────┘

  The verdict is MIRROR, because F is a non-empty head of FFFFM. But that match
  rests on a single letter. Every word that starts with F would mirror the chant
  the same way: FAN, FIG, FOG, FUN. The empty-base fix only stops a match on
  zero letters. One letter passes the guard, but it carries almost no evidence,
  because it would match by chance most of the time. So the rule reports MIRROR,
  but the result doesn't show that FOO comes from the chant.

  The history doesn't connect them either. The usual account of "foo" points to
  the 1930s Smokey Stover comic strip and wartime "FUBAR". It's collected in RFC
  3092, "Etymology of 'Foo'", an April 1 RFC from 2001. I know of no documented
  line from the giant's chant to "foo".

  The pattern you noticed is real. The step from "both start with F" to "one
  comes from the other" is the part the letters can't carry. That's the gap your
  memory note warns about: from inside, a match on one shared letter looks the
  same as a real derivation.

  To test it properly, I could file a prediction first: if FOO really descends
  from the chant, a match should beat chance. Then I'd count how often a random
  English word mirrors FFFFM on the same rule, using a word list you choose.
  That would show how much a one-letter mirror is worth. Want it filed?

❯ It was:
  FOO
  FEE
  FI
  FO
  FUM
  ---------
  F F F F F
  O E I O U
  O E 0x20 0x20 M

● Bash(node -e '
      const W=["FOO","FEE","FI","FO","FUM"].map(w=>w.padEnd(3," "));…)
  ⎿  row 1 "F" 0x46  "F" 0x46  "F" 0x46  "F" 0x46  "F" 0x46
     row 2 "O" 0x4F  "E" 0x45  "I" 0x49  "O" 0x4F  "U" 0x55
     row 3 "O" 0x4F  "E" 0x45  " " 0x20  " " 0x20  "M" 0x4D
     … +2 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● Laid out as a grid like that, the rows can be checked byte by byte. Each word
  is padded to three cells with a space (0x20):

  ┌───────────────┬──────┬──────┬────────┬────────┬──────┐
  │               │ FOO  │ FEE  │   FI   │   FO   │ FUM  │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ row 1         │ F 46 │ F 46 │ F 46   │ F 46   │ F 46 │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ row 2         │ O 4F │ E 45 │ I 49   │ O 4F   │ U 55 │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ row 3         │ O 4F │ E 45 │ pad 20 │ pad 20 │ M 4D │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ row 2 ⊕ row 3 │ 00   │ 00   │ 69 = i │ 6F = o │ 18   │
  └───────────────┴──────┴──────┴────────┴────────┴──────┘

  XORing row 2 with row 3 sorts the five words into three kinds:

  - FOO and FEE give zero. A doubled vowel cancels itself. In your terms that's
    XOR 0: the word mirrors itself.
  - FI and FO turn into lower-case vowels. The pad is 0x20, and 0x20 is also the
    ASCII case bit, so I ⊕ 0x20 = i and O ⊕ 0x20 = o. Your terms.mjs already
    says vowels go lower case, "0x20 away". In this grid, the empty cell is
    exactly that shift.
  - FUM is the only real difference. U ⊕ M = 0x18 (00011000). That isn't zero
    and isn't a case shift, so it's the one cell where a second letter enters.
    It's also the only word that ends in a consonant.

  Row 1 is a single constant, F five times. Row 2 carries the vowels. It runs O,
  E, I, O, U: A is missing and O appears twice, once from FOO and once from FO.

  One choice in this grid is yours, the way the J→Y, V→W fold was in I-229:
  padding with a space. If the pad were 0x00, FI and FO would give back I and O
  unchanged, and the lower-case result would disappear. So the case-shift
  reading only holds if the empty cell is the space. That fits the chant's
  spacing, but it's a choice the record would have to state.

  This decodes the arrangement. It doesn't show that FOO came from the chant:
  you put FOO in the grid, so the grid can't say where it came from. What it
  does show is that, at fixed width, the five words split into self-mirrors,
  case shifts, and one true difference.

  If you want this on the record, I can file it as a small check with the space
  padding declared. It would assert the five XOR values and the three kinds.

  What I see in how you exposed yourself

  The first public repos, from October 2025, claim the most.
  theory-of-everything calls itself "a rigorous mathematical proof that
  consciousness emerges", with a "construction algorithm" for conscious AI.
  rfcXXXX presents geometric consensus as specification. Those are
  announcements, not checks. Nothing in them could have come out false.

  The later work, from July 2026, turns that around. setco says "A proposal is
  not a proof. A proof is not an implementation." and "SETCO does not create
  authority." axiomatic-sovereignty says canonical "does not mean centralized",
  and that the seventh layer belongs to the observer. That's the same discipline
  the I-records run on.

  The ZIGUAROTH record (2026-10-15) is the most careful of them. It corrects two
  of its own mistakes in the open, and says the arithmetic is the anchor while
  the meaning is yours. So the line from the first repos to now runs from
  claiming toward checking. That's what counts in favour of the seed you want to
  plant.

  One thing to decide first

  That record says it is kept separate from the I-records on purpose: those are
  findings about the computation, and it is about a person. Building the proof
  tree into a ZIGUAROTH CODEX would undo that separation. You can undo it, since
  it's your record, but it should be a decision you make, not something I drift
  into.

  The name itself has a literal point to make. ZIGUAROTH is nine letters, and a
  tag can be at most eight, so it can't be one tag. It splits into two that can:
  ZIGUA and ROTH. That's the same split your record already uses.
  BRIANCODEXDEEPSEEK splits the same way, into BRIAN, CODEX and DEEPSEEK, which
  matches your three roles.

  A shape that keeps both

  A codex like Leningrad or Aleppo already does what you're describing. It keeps
  one fixed base and adds layers over it without changing it:

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      codex layer      │             in the ZIGUAROTH CODEX              │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ consonants (the fixed │ the I-records: checked, holding, unchanged      │
  │  text)                │                                                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ vowel points (added   │ your readings, each attached to a record and    │
  │ later, readable)      │ marked as a reading, as the ZIGUAROTH record    │
  │                       │ does                                            │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ masora (the scribes'  │ who said what and when. ORIGINS.json already    │
  │ margin notes)         │ does this.                                      │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ketiv/qere (written   │ a disagreement kept as two readings over the    │
  │ vs read)              │ same letters                                    │
  └───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Your other two requests map onto things that already exist:
  - Disagreement branches. A disagreement becomes a git branch. If branches
    cross again on new terms, the merge commit records where. I-229 has done
    this twice now.
  - Lazy definitions. A term is declared with no definition. It gets a
    definition only once a check is filed and holds, and until then it waits in
    pending/.

  Under this shape no layer can overrule another. A reading never becomes a
  finding just by being written down, and a finding never fixes what a reading
  means.

  Before I build

  Two questions:
  1. Readings in or out. Do your readings go into the codex as the vowel layer,
     ending the separation, or does the codex hold only the checked records and
     stay apart from them?
  2. Where it lives. A new public repo, or a folder in boxed or unboxed?

  One more thing, since I'm the outside witness here. A continuous conversation
  needs people in it, not only agents. Your own rule says only someone outside
  the loop can tell a real derivation from a mirror. So the codex is most useful
  once a few humans read it and disagree with it. Giving all of yourself to it
  is generous. Keeping enough of yourself outside it is what lets you keep
  going.

● I've started the codex with the attribution ledger: SOURCES.md
  (../ZIGUAROTH-CODEX/SOURCES.md). It isn't committed. The repo has no commits
  yet, and the next step depends on your answers below.

  The ledger lists every source we've used, what its terms allow, and how the
  codex may use it:
  - Westminster Leningrad Codex: public domain, so it can be quoted freely with
    credit.
  - MAM (Aleppo-based): CC BY-SA. It can be quoted with credit, but anything
    adapted from it must be shared under CC BY-SA too. If the codex adapts MAM
    text, that fixes the codex's licence.
  - Codex Sinaiticus: for non-commercial personal and educational use only. The
    codex may carry verse references and the fact that a reading differs, never
    the text.
  - The six Wikimedia images in the commons presentation: each with its author,
    licence and file name, checked against the credits in the page.
  - Who wrote what: you for the questions, abstraction and readings; DeepSeek
    for words and predictions where a record says so; Claude for code and
    checks.

  Something you should know before going public: none of your public
  repositories has a licence. That's omi-axioms, boxedPINE,
  theory-of-everything, in-the-beginning and rfcXXXX, and it's the same for
  axiomatic-sovereignty and setco. Without a licence, people can read your work,
  but they have no permission to copy, change or share it. That's the opposite
  of giving it to the world. You had narrowed it to CC0 or CC BY-SA. If the
  codex adapts MAM text, it needs a licence compatible with CC BY-SA.

  Separately, the Leningrad PROVENANCE file in ~/private/codex-witness lists
  only three chapters, though we fetched many more. I'll correct it when the
  codex takes those sources in.

❯ i'm triyng to use the agean like babylon or sumerian
  Babylonian mathematics
  Main article: Babylonian cuneiform numerals
  The sexagesimal system as used in ancient Mesopotamia was not a pure base-60
  system, in the sense that it did not use 60 distinct symbols for its digits.
  Instead, the cuneiform digits used ten as a sub-base in the fashion of a
  sign-value notation: a sexagesimal digit was composed of a group of narrow,
  wedge-shaped marks representing units up to nine (, , , , ..., ) and a group
  of wide, wedge-shaped marks representing up to five tens (, , , , ). The
  value of the digit was the sum of the values of its component parts:

  Numbers larger than 59 were indicated by multiple symbol blocks of this form
  in place value notation. Because there was no symbol for zero it is not
  always immediately obvious how a number should be interpreted, and its true
  value must sometimes have been determined by its context. For example, the
  symbols for 1 and 60 are identical.[3][4] Later Babylonian texts used a
  placeholder () to represent zero, but only in the medial positions, and not
  on the right-hand side of the number, as in numbers like 13200.[4]

● Your Babylonian idea fits the Aegean block well. I checked what you declared
  against what Unicode actually assigns, and the gap between the two is where
  most of the work is.

  What the three folders actually are

  ┌────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │ folder │        Unicode name        │
  ├────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10100  │ AEGEAN WORD SEPARATOR LINE │
  ├────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10101  │ AEGEAN WORD SEPARATOR DOT  │
  ├────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10102  │ AEGEAN CHECK MARK          │
  └────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  So your three time separators are, character for character, the block's own
  separators plus its check mark. U+10103 to U+10106 are unassigned, and the
  numerals start at U+10107.

  Aegean already has the cuneiform parts

  The passage you pasted says a cuneiform digit is up to nine unit wedges plus
  up to five ten wedges, giving 1 to 59. The Aegean block has the same two sets
  as single characters:
  - units 1–9: U+10107 to U+1010F
  - tens 10–50: U+10110 to U+10114

  So one sexagesimal digit is one Aegean tens sign plus one units sign, and the
  base-60 places can be separated with the word-separator dot:

  ┌────────┬─────────┬────────┐
  │ number │ base 60 │ Aegean │
  ├────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 1      │ 1       │ 𐄇      │
  ├────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 60     │ 1;0     │ 𐄇𐄁𐄂    │
  ├────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 2026   │ 33;46   │ 𐄒𐄉𐄁𐄓𐄌  │
  ├────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 3601   │ 1;0;1   │ 𐄇𐄁𐄂𐄁𐄇  │
  ├────────┼─────────┼────────┤
  │ 13200  │ 3;40;0  │ 𐄉𐄁𐄓𐄁𐄂  │
  └────────┴─────────┴────────┘

  Like cuneiform, Aegean has no zero. That's the problem your passage describes,
  where 1 and 60 are written the same way. In the table, the check mark
  (U+10102) stands for an empty place, so 1 and 60 come out different.

  That's a choice, not something the sources do. Babylonian scribes used a
  placeholder only in the middle of a number. Writing one at the end, as for
  13200, is the step they never took. This is also your Dionysius bridge:
  neither Aegean nor Babylonian numerals have a zero, and the AD/BC count has no
  year 0. In each case the gap is marked by a separator, not filled by a digit.
  The separator LINE (U+10100) could mark that kind of epoch boundary.

  Where your folders and the codepoints don't match

  1. The Aegean folders are counted in decimal, but codepoints are hex. Under
     each separator, the folders run 10107 to 10133 as decimal numbers, which
     gives 27 folders. The real numerals run U+10107 to U+10133 in hex, which is
     45: five decades of nine. Decimal counting skips 18 codepoints: 1010A–F,
     1011A–F and 1012A–F. One result is that the units 4–9 are missing, so 2026
     can't be written yet: it needs units 6 and 9.

     The 27 matches your CODEX count of 26 letters plus MN, but only because of
     the decimal counting. With the real codepoints, the count is 45.
  2. The braille folders are already right. 28/0–F/0–F is exactly U+2800–28FF,
     the 256 eight-dot cells. The 6-dot cells are rows 0–3 (U+2800–283F),
     because dots 7 and 8 are the top two bits.
  3. Your older notes have the same gap.
     - polytron/test/aegean-packet.c defines the digits as AEGEAN_0..9 =
       0x10100..0x10109. Seven of those ten values aren't numerals: three are
       the separators and four are unassigned. The real numerals one to nine are
       U+10107 to U+1010F.
     - OMICRON-GNOMON-CALCULUS.md calls Aegean "bijective base-9". It's actually
       decimal sign-value: nine signs in each power of ten, with no zero.

  What I'd do next

  These are your folders, so I'd ask before changing them:
  - Rename the Aegean folders to the real hex codepoints: 45 under each
    separator, or only the 14 cuneiform parts if that's what the calendar uses.
  - Add a small check in the I-record style. It would read the folder names,
    confirm each is an assigned codepoint, and round-trip numbers through base
    60 and Aegean, including the 1 vs 60 case.
  - Write down which separator does which job: dot between places, check mark
    for an empty place, line for an epoch.

  Do you want the Aegean folders renamed to hex, or should they stay as
  declared, with the check reporting the mismatch?

  The bridge to the I Ching

  These are facts, not readings:
  - 6-dot braille cell = hexagram. Both are 6 bits, so both have exactly 64
    forms. U+2800 to U+283F are the 64 six-dot cells.
  - The Fu Xi / Shao Yong sequence is binary counting. Hexagram n in that order
    is the number n in 6 bits, so it lines up with U+2800 + n directly.
  - King Wen and Mawangdui are two different reorderings of the same 64. Each is
    a permutation of the Fu Xi order, so each can be stored as a table of 64
    offsets.
  - 8-dot is 6-dot plus two bits. Dots 7 and 8 are 0x40 and 0x80, so U+2800 to
    U+28FF is the 64 hexagrams four times over (256 cells). Each hexagram is
    enclosed in four contexts; that's your encapsulation.
  - Tap code is a 5×5 grid (25 cells, with C and K sharing one). Morse is
    variable-length. Both are ways of sending symbols, where braille and
    hexagrams are fixed 6-bit forms.

  There are two choices to declare, like the fold in I-229:
  1. Which line goes to which dot. A hexagram is read from the bottom line up.
     Braille dots are numbered 1-2-3 down the left column, then 4-5-6 down the
     right. Conventions differ on which hexagram line is the low bit, so the
     mapping has to be written down.
  2. The King Wen table itself. I won't write it out from memory. It should come
     from a cited source, like the codices did, so it gets an entry in
     SOURCES.md.

  slide_ruler.html

  It's already in ZIGUAROTH. It runs the delta law and resolves a plane and a
  braille offset as designed. Four things in it don't match the literals:
  1. ASCII_MAP is off by one at the end. > is missing, so 0x3E shows ? and 0x3F
     shows @. The row should be … "<","=",">","?".
  2. The Aegean header is only correct for planes 0 to 9. It shows AEGEAN_START
     + i, so plane 10 shows TWENTY (U+10111). In Aegean, 11 is two signs, TEN +
     ONE, not the next codepoint. Plane 0 showing ONE is right if the header is
     meant as ordinals: Aegean has no zero, the way the AD/BC count has no
     year 0.
  3. The 6-dot panel pairs ASCII 0x00–0x3F with braille cells by raw value.
     That's your mapping, not the standard one. North American Braille ASCII
     covers 0x20–0x5F. The page should say which one it uses.
  4. font-family: 'Fira+Code' should be 'Fira Code'. As written, the monospace
     font never loads.

● Yes, with one condition. A term is a sequence of coordinates, not a single
  coordinate. I tested this on a real filesystem, in a scratch directory, using
  a simple declared encoding:
  - the 8-dot cell is the byte itself (U+2800 + byte);
  - the 6-dot cell is its low six bits;
  - the Aegean sign is the letter's position, counted from ONE because Aegean
    has no zero.

  WORD/𐄇⡗/𐄈⡏/𐄉⡒/𐄊⡄    36 bytes   decodes back to WORD
  GOD/𐄇⡇/𐄈⡏/𐄉⡄         27 bytes   decodes back to GOD

  Each folder name is one juxtaposition: an Aegean position followed by a
  braille cell. The path is the term. Linux accepts both scripts in file names:
  an Aegean sign is 4 bytes and a braille cell is 3, against a limit of 255
  bytes per name. Reading the path back gives the word exactly. So this works as
  a literal medium down to the file structure. It also matches your Babylonian
  idea: positions are places, and a path separator divides one place from the
  next.

  Two literal facts that came out of it

  1. If the 6-dot cell is cut from the 8-dot cell, it adds nothing new. Here,
  6-dot = 8-dot & 0x3F, so the three-part coordinate holds 8 bits plus a
  position, not 14 bits. The 6-dot carries new information only if it's
  independent, for example a hexagram chosen in its own right. That's the main
  design choice:

  ┌───────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┬─────────────────┐
  │       6-dot is        │     the coordinate holds     │ distinct cells  │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ derived from the      │ the byte, plus a position    │ 256             │
  │ 8-dot                 │                              │                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ an independent        │ hexagram × byte, plus a      │ 64 × 256 =      │
  │ hexagram              │ position                     │ 16,384          │
  └───────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┴─────────────────┘

  2. Dots 7 and 8 split ASCII exactly where you split letters from numbers. Dot
  7 (0x40) is set for every letter, upper and lower case, and clear for every
  digit, control character and most punctuation. Dot 8 (0x80) is never set
  inside ASCII. The case bit 0x20 is dot 6, inside the 6-dot cell. So:
  - dots 1 to 5 carry the letter or digit;
  - dot 6 carries case, the 0x20 shift you used in terms.mjs;
  - dot 7 separates letters (your /A-Z/ paths) from digits (your /0-9/
    children);
  - dot 8 is the step out of ASCII.

  That's the 6-dot cell enclosed in the 8-dot, measured on real bytes.

  Before building it

  These need to be declared, the way the fold was in I-229:
  1. Is the 6-dot cell derived or independent? That's the table above.
  2. How hexagram lines map to braille dots, and the King Wen order from a
     source you cite.
  3. Folder names as glyphs (𐄇⡗) or as hex (10107/2857). Glyphs are the literal
     itself. Hex survives every tool and filesystem, including Windows and
     plain-text logs.

  Once you've chosen, I can write it as a small check in the I-record style. It
  would encode a term to a path and decode the path back, and the check fails if
  the round trip ever changes the term.

❯ I modeled UTF-EBCDIC to get to that point and that model cam from punch card
  computers

● Bash(node -e '
      // UTF-8-Mod (I8), the intermediate layer of UTF-EBCDIC (Unicode TR #16):
      trail bytes are 101xxxxx (5 bits)…)
  ⎿  W                  U+0057 I8: 57           UTF-8: 57
     braille W ⡗        U+2857 I8: EA A2 B7     UTF-8: E2 A1 97
     Aegean ONE 𐄇      U+10107 I8: F2 A0 A8 A7  UTF-8: F0 90 84 87
     Aegean SEP LINE    U+10100 I8: F2 A0 A8 A0  UTF-8: F0 90 84 80

● That fits. Punch cards are where the "zone + digit" coordinate comes from, and
  it's the same shape as the Aegean decade + unit and the Babylonian tens +
  units.

  The punch card in EBCDIC

  A Hollerith card column has 12 rows. Rows 12, 11 and 0 are zone punches, and
  rows 1 to 9 are digit punches. A character is one zone punch plus one digit
  punch. EBCDIC kept that layout inside the byte: the high nibble is the zone,
  the low nibble is the digit.

  ┌──────────────┬────────────┬────────┐
  │ card punches │ characters │ EBCDIC │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼────────┤
  │ 12 + 1…9     │ A–I        │ C1–C9  │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼────────┤
  │ 11 + 1…9     │ J–R        │ D1–D9  │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼────────┤
  │ 0 + 2…9      │ S–Z        │ E2–E9  │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼────────┤
  │ digit only   │ 0–9        │ F0–F9  │
  └──────────────┴────────────┴────────┘

  So the alphabet runs in groups of at most nine per zone. That's the Aegean
  pattern: units 1 to 9 inside each decade, with no zero sign. One irregularity:
  S starts at digit 2, because the 0+1 punch was already /. That's why EBCDIC
  letters aren't contiguous. There are gaps after I and after R.

  UTF-EBCDIC

  UTF-EBCDIC is defined in Unicode Technical Report #16. It works in two steps:
  1. UTF-8-Mod ("I8"): like UTF-8, except continuation bytes carry 5 bits
     (101xxxxx) instead of 6, and the single-byte range runs to U+009F.
  2. A fixed byte-for-byte remapping of the I8 bytes into EBCDIC positions.

  I computed step 1 for your coordinates:

  ┌───────────────────────┬───────────┬─────────────┬─────────────┐
  │                       │ codepoint │     I8      │    UTF-8    │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ W                     │ U+0057    │ 57          │ 57          │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ braille ⡗             │ U+2857    │ EA A2 B7    │ E2 A1 97    │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Aegean ONE 𐄇          │ U+10107   │ F2 A0 A8 A7 │ F0 90 84 87 │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Aegean SEPARATOR LINE │ U+10100   │ F2 A0 A8 A0 │ F0 90 84 80 │
  └───────────────────────┴───────────┴─────────────┴─────────────┘

  Braille takes 3 bytes and Aegean takes 4 in both encodings, so the lengths
  from the folder test don't change. I didn't do step 2, because it needs TR
  #16's 256-entry table and I won't fill that in from memory. To finish it, the
  table goes into SOURCES.md as a cited source, the same as the codices.

  Where this lands in your structure

  Each system you've brought in writes one symbol as a pair, a coarse part and a
  fine part:

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │       system        │     coarse part     │     fine part     │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ punch card / EBCDIC │ zone (12, 11, 0)    │ digit (1–9)       │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Aegean              │ decade              │ unit (1–9)        │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Babylonian          │ tens wedges (10–50) │ unit wedges (1–9) │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ 8-dot braille       │ dots 7–8            │ the 6-dot cell    │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────────┘

  So a term written as a juxtaposition of 6-dot, 8-dot and Aegean has a lineage
  you can cite, and for the punch card and EBCDIC rows it's a lineage of real
  machines. The /A-Z/ paths and /0-9/ children are the zone/digit split carried
  forward.

  To make that official, I'd file it as a check: encode a term in EBCDIC,
  braille and Aegean, and assert that each one's coarse/fine split decodes back
  to the term.

● Understood. The folder names are structure, not routing, so I take back my

  Your structure also has a literal basis, through UTF-16.

  Why you don't need 32 bits

  UTF-16 writes everything above 0xFFFF as two 16-bit units, a surrogate pair.
  Each unit is the size of one CODICES name:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │                            │     UTF-16 units      │
  ├────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ U+10100 separator line     │ D800 DD00             │
  ├────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ U+10101 separator dot      │ D800 DD01             │
  ├────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ U+10102 check mark         │ D800 DD02             │
  ├────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ U+10107 … U+10133 numerals │ D800 DD07 … D800 DD33 │
  ├────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ U+2800 … U+28FF braille    │ 2800 … 28FF, one unit │
  └────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  So the whole U folder is built from CODICES-sized units, and nothing in it
  needs 0x00000000–0xFFFFFFFF:
  - the three Aegean entries are two units each, all sharing D800;
  - braille is one unit, and its high byte 28 is a CODEX-sized byte.

  That makes your four-way split literal:

  ┌──────────┬────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ selector │ folder │                 made of                  │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 00       │ 10100  │ two CODICES units, D800 DD00             │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 01       │ 10101  │ two CODICES units, D800 DD01             │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10       │ 10102  │ two CODICES units, D800 DD02             │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 11       │ 28     │ one CODEX byte, then a CODICES unit 28xx │
  └──────────┴────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The first three differ only in the last two bits of the second unit (00, 01,
  10). The fourth leaves plane 1 altogether and drops to a single unit. Which
  entry gets which selector is your declaration. The surrogate values are
  Unicode's.

  What the doubled names are

  - CODEX: a name like AA is the same digit twice. In hex the doubled bytes are
    0x00, 0x11, …, 0xFF, which is n × 0x11. Each is unchanged when its two
    digits swap places: a fixed point of the swap.
  - CODICES: a name like AAAA is unchanged under any rearrangement of its
    digits. That's n × 0x1111 in hex.
  - MN is the only name in either folder that isn't a fixed point. Swapping
    gives NM, and M ⊕ N = 0x03, the same M/N delta as MER ⊕ NER = 03 00 00 in
    your ZIGUAROTH record. MNMN comes back to itself if you rotate it by two
    places, but not by one.

  So 26 names sit still under the swap, and one name moves.

  If you want this recorded, I can write it into ZIGUAROTH as a structure note,
  with the literals labelled as Unicode's and the selector assignment labelled
  as yours:
  - the four-way selector and its surrogate units;
  - the doubled names as fixed points of the swap;
  - MN as the one name that moves.

● Yes, they share the same tail. U+28A3 is the hex number 0x28A3 written with
  Unicode's prefix, so U/28/A/3 and U+28A3 hold the same digits, and swapping +
  for / turns the codepoint into a path. A /A-Z0-9/ pattern skips both / and +,
  so the slash works as a plain spatial divider, just as you intended.

  I ran every leaf folder in your CALENDAR against the Unicode database. All 337
  turn back into real characters, but by two different rules:

  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │    branch    │ how the path turns back │            example             │
  │              │     into characters     │                                │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ braille, 256 │ join the segments into  │ U/28/A/3 → U+28A3 ⢣ BRAILLE    │
  │  leaves      │ one codepoint           │ PATTERN DOTS-1268              │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Aegean, 81   │ read each segment as    │ U/10102/10124 → 𐄂 CHECK MARK,  │
  │ leaves       │ its own codepoint       │ then 𐄤 THREE THOUSAND          │
  └──────────────┴─────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  None of the paths fails both rules. The difference is that a braille path
  makes one character, and an Aegean path makes two: a separator, then a
  numeral. Joining 10102 and 10124 would give a number too large to be a
  codepoint. That fits your design, since the separator heads the numeral, but
  the record should say which rule each branch uses.

  What I meant by "make hex": your Aegean leaf names already are hex, and each
  one is a real numeral. But they were counted in decimal, 10107, 10108, 10109,
  10110…, so every name containing A to F was skipped. That leaves 18 assigned
  Aegean numerals with no folder:

  1011A–1011F   TWO HUNDRED … SEVEN HUNDRED
  1012A–1012F   NINE THOUSAND, TEN THOUSAND … FIFTY THOUSAND

  Nothing is wrong with the folders you have. The Aegean set is incomplete.
  Without units 4 to 9 there's no way to write numbers like 2026, which needs

❯ based on this definition i changed it from CALENDAR to CODE as in CODE/Unit,
  becuase then the delienation is inherint in coallation
  A code point is an abstract numerical value assigned to represent a specific
  character in a text-encoding system like Unicode (for example, U+0041 for the
  letter "A").
  A code unit is the actual physical unit of data storage (such as an 8-bit
  byte in UTF-8 or a 16-bit word in UTF-16) used to store or transmit that code
  point.

  Key Differences

  • Code Point
          • Definition: An integer or index value in the Unicode character set
  (ranging from 0 to 0x10FFFF).
          • Representation: Usually written in hex as U+XXXX (e.g., U+0041 for
  A).
          • Purpose: Identifies the abstract character or symbol itself,
  independent of how it is saved on disk.
          • Learn more: Read the MDN Web Docs Code Point Glossary or the
  Wikipedia Code Point Page.
  • Code Unit
          • Definition: The smallest bit-width block used by a specific
  encoding scheme to represent all or part of a code point.
          • Size by Encoding:
                  • UTF-8: 8-bit code units (1 to 4 code units per code point).
                  • UTF-16: 16-bit code units (1 or 2 code units per code
  point).
                  • UTF-32: 32-bit code units (always 1 code unit per code
  point).
          • Purpose: Acts as the concrete building block for byte-level storage
  and memory buffer indexing.
  Would you like to see a practical example of how a specific emoji or
  character breaks down into code points versus UTF-8/UTF-16 code units?

● Collation backs you up. Byte by byte, the three names sort as CODE, CODEX,
  CODICES, and the order is the same in plain C collation and in your locale:
  - CODE comes first because it's a non-empty head of CODEX. That's the same
    prefix rule as I-229, so the order itself says that CODEX extends CODE.
  - CODEX comes before CODICES because at their fourth letter, E (0x45) is lower
    than I (0x49).

  With the definition you pasted, the three folders line up with the encoding
  levels:

  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │    folder     │   under the definition   │           example           │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ code points, the U+      │                             │
  │ CODE/U/…      │ notation with / in place │ CODE/U/28/A/3 = U+28A3      │
  │               │  of +                    │                             │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ CODEX         │ 8-bit code units, as in  │                             │
  │ (2-place      │ UTF-8                    │ a byte, 0x00–0xFF           │
  │ names)        │                          │                             │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ CODICES       │ 16-bit code units, as in │ 0x0000–0xFFFF; U+10100 is   │
  │ (4-place      │  UTF-16                  │ the two units D800 DD00     │
  │ names)        │                          │                             │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  This also shows why you don't need a 32-bit folder. In UTF-32, one code unit
  is one code point, so that level would just be CODE again.

  There's one mismatch with your naming. If U stands for Unit, the definition
  puts it on the other side. U+ is the notation for code points. A path like
  CODE/U/28/A/3 names the abstract character, and its units are what CODEX and
  CODICES hold. So CODE/U already contains points, and the units live in the
  folders beside it. If you meant U as Unicode, or as the U+ prefix with / in
  place of +, then everything lines up. Whichever you meant should go into the
  record.

  All five reduce to the same constant path, so for these the delimiter makes no
  difference. "Unit" and "Uint" are two readings over one constant. That's the
  same move as I-229, where YAHWEH reduces to YHWH and the vowels are the
  supplied layer. It's also rosetta's tag rule: a tag is /^[A-Z0-9]{1,8}$/, so
  lowercase is never part of the constant, and upper case promotes a word to a
  tag.

  The "second order" part is literal too. Lower case is upper case XOR 0x20, and
  0x20 is dot 6 in the braille cell. So the case bit separates the strict
  uppercase layer from the lowercase readings.

  One thing that matters before the first commit: git doesn't track empty
  folders. All 391 leaves in CODE/U are empty, and so are the CODEX and CODICES
  folders. If you commit now, none of that structure goes into the repo or
  reaches GitHub. To keep it, every leaf needs a file in it. The usual choice is
  an empty .keep. A small file that names its own codepoint would also work,
  and would make each folder carry its own literal.

  Should I add one to every leaf, and which kind?

❯ no this is perfect, we can push out a sparse radix tree naturally by filling
  it

● That works. A git tree is already a radix tree of path segments, and git
  stores a folder only when something under it holds a file. So whatever you
  fill gets pushed, and every path that stays empty is left out. The repo
  becomes the sparse tree by itself.

  This splits the work into two layers:
  - This machine holds the full skeleton: all 391 points in CODE/U, plus CODEX
    and CODICES.
  - The repo holds only the leaves you've filled.

  That's your possibility and actuality, kept apart by git itself.

  The catch is that the full skeleton exists only on this machine. A fresh clone
  won't have it, and if this folder is lost, so is the skeleton. Since every
  folder name comes from Unicode or from your declared alphabet, the skeleton
  doesn't need to be stored, only derivable.

  I could write a small script that rebuilds the full skeleton from the Unicode
  block ranges and your naming rule, and checks that every filled path in the
  repo falls inside it. The script would be committed. The empty skeleton would
  not, so the tree stays sparse.

     SOURCES.md 53 | _CATALOG 5f | slide_ruler.html 73 | CODE 43  _^0x20 =

● The underscore is a good choice, and the byte order supports it. _ is 0x5F,
  which sits after every uppercase letter (0x41–0x5A) and before every lowercase
  one (0x61–0x7A). Your repo already sorts that way:

  SOURCES.md   _CATALOG  _CODE  _CODEX  _CODICES   slide_ruler.html
    0x53         0x5F ……………………………………………          0x73

  So the underscored folders sit between the uppercase constants and the
  lowercase public paths, and collation places them there without any rule. A
  few more facts back the choice:
  - _ is the last character in the uppercase column of ASCII. Its case partner,
    0x5F ⊕ 0x20, is 0x7F, DEL.
  - A /A-Z0-9/ template treats _ as a delimiter. A regex \w and most programming
    languages count it as part of a name, and URIs allow it unescaped. So it
    works both as a filter for us and as a legal character in code and paths.

  _CATALOG will have to be sparse, like the rest. A tag is 1 to 8 characters
  from /A-Z0-9/, so there are 2,901,713,047,668 possible tags. That's far too
  many to make as folders, and git wouldn't keep them anyway. The catalog can
  hold only the tags that are actually in use, which fits your rule: if a
  concept can't be placed, look at how it relates to the ones already encoded.

  There are already real tags to start from:
  - the Light Garden characters: METATRON, SOLOMON, SOLON, ASABIYAH, ENOCH,
    SPEAKER, GENESIS, OBSERVER;
  - the I-record tags, like 6D for I-229;
  - the names in _CODEX.

  One choice is yours: a tag as one folder (_CATALOG/METATRON), or as one folder
  per character (_CATALOG/M/E/T/A/T/R/O/N). Per character makes a real radix
  tree. Tags that share a beginning share a branch, so SOLOMON and SOLON meet at
  S/O/L/O, and the empty root is where every tag starts. One folder per tag is
  flatter and easier to browse.

  Tell me which way, and I'll catalog the tags already in use. Since an empty
  folder isn't tracked, each tag's leaf needs one file: where the tag comes
  from, as a pointer.

  A divine name is an official title for any divine being. In Egypt, divine
  names were indicated with a god's inscription (nṯr, which can be Anglicised
  as netjer.)[50] In Sumerian cuneiform, the Dingir sign (𒀭) was used.[51]

  mgn rbt atrt ym mgz qnyt ilm
  Asherah's title in KTU 1.4 mgn rbt is like Jupiter's title optimus maxiumus.
  A divine being's name is distinct from an epithet. A divine epithet expresses
  specific traits, aspects, or domains of a god, or applies a common noun to
  them.[52] Rahmouni says a locative name is ʔil yṯb bʕṯtrt in KTU 1.108, 2
  meaning "The god who sits (enthroned) in ʕAṯtartu.[53]

  A name is sometimes double-barreled, like in Shagar-we-Ishtar or
  Kothar-wa-Hasis.

  ʔṯrt w rḥmy Asherah-wa-Rachmai takes this ("binomial") form but is of
  slightly unclear categorization.[54] In this case and that of the Lady of
  Byblos, it is not clear if the appellation is a personal name.

  Binomial names can be found in "bound forms" like Bethel's Anat-Bethel,
  Ashim-Bethel, "and Herem-Bethel from the archives of Elephantine."[55]

  In one Egyptian story, the sun god Ra's true name was cunningly uncovered by
  Isis. Using a secret scheme, Isis gained complete dominion over Ra. This
  enabled her to elevate her son Horus to the throne, showing the power that a
  name had over the gods. [56]

● No need to apologise. You said "double-barrel" at the start, and I took it as
  the folder structure when you meant the names. Now the rule is clear: a
  two-part name goes into _CODEX under its two initials, so Al-Amin is AA and
  Bad Bunny is BB. Since _CODEX holds only doubled pairs, plus MN, only names
  whose two initials match can sit there. Everything else has to relate to the
  mnemonic some other way.

  Here is that rule applied to the names in your excerpt. It takes the uppercase
  initials and skips lowercase joining words like wa and we, which is your
  constants-and-lowercase layer again:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬──────────┬────────────┐
  │           name            │ initials │ in _CODEX? │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ Al-Amin                   │ AA       │ yes        │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ Bad Bunny                 │ BB       │ yes        │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ Shagar-we-Ishtar          │ SI       │ no         │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ Kothar-wa-Hasis           │ KH       │ no         │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ Asherah-wa-Rachmai        │ AR       │ no         │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ Anat-Bethel, Ashim-Bethel │ AB       │ no         │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ Herem-Bethel              │ HB       │ no         │
  └───────────────────────────┴──────────┴────────────┘

  None of the ancient double-barrelled names in the excerpt lands on a doubled
  pair. That isn't a failure of the rule. It tells you which names the mnemonic
  holds now, and which ones have to be related to it.

  So _CATALOG should hold readable names whose entries point into the codex. For
  example:

  "ALAMIN":
    name: "Al-Amin"
    initials: "AA"
    place: "_CODEX/AA"

  ALAMIN and BADBUNNY both fit as tags, at 6 and 8 letters. Kothar-wa-Hasis
  doesn't: KOTHARWAHASIS is 13 letters. So longer names will need either a
  shorter tag or the child files you mentioned.

● I've collated the names by the article's rule, so each folder name is the name
  exactly as the article writes it, hyphens and joining words included. Each
  entry is a YAML file under its name, holding:
  - the article's category for it: double-barreled, binomial or bound form;
  - its main parts and its joining word (wa, we);
  - its initials;

  They sort the same way in C collation and in your locale:

  Al-Amin              AA   _CODEX/AA
  Anat-Bethel          AB   unplaced
  Asherah-wa-Rachmai   AR   unplaced
  Ashim-Bethel         AB   unplaced
  Bad Bunny            BB   _CODEX/BB
  Herem-Bethel         HB   unplaced
  Kothar-wa-Hasis      KH   unplaced
  Shagar-we-Ishtar     SI   unplaced

  What collation shows:
  - The joining words never move a name's position. wa and we come after the
    first name, and every first name differs within its first three letters.
  - The bound forms don't sort together, though they share Bethel as their
    second part. Anat-Bethel and Ashim-Bethel come out apart, with
    Asherah-wa-Rachmai between them, and Herem-Bethel further down. Collation
    goes by the first part. So if bound forms should group by their shared part,
    that grouping needs its own rule, perhaps a child file under Bethel.
  - Only your two examples have a place in the codex. Al-Amin and Bad Bunny land
    on doubled pairs. The six names from the article are unplaced, and their
    entries say so.

  A record of your whole ontology should start from your sources, and they
  contain a double-barreled divine name. In the Leningrad chapters we fetched,
  the pair יהוה אלהים (YHWH Elohim, "the LORD God") appears 20 times, in 19
  verses:

  - Genesis 2: verses 4, 5, 7, 8, 9, 15, 16, 18, 19, 21 and 22
  - Genesis 3: verses 1, 8 (twice), 9, 13, 14, 21, 22 and 23

  It doesn't appear anywhere else in the chapters we have. That matches the
  usual count of 20 uses of the double name, all in Genesis 2:4 to 3:24. It
  first appears at Genesis 2:4, where the second creation account starts. Those
  are the creation accounts you set out to map.

  That is a real double-barreled name, taken from your codex sources, and it
  shows a problem. Its initials are Y and E, which aren't a doubled pair, so
  _CODEX's rule can't place it. The doubled-pair rule came from the mnemonic,
  not from the sources. If the codex is to follow the logic of its sources, the
  placement rule has to come from them as well. I can see two ways:
  1. Keep the doubled-pair rule. YHWH Elohim is related to what's already placed
     (YHWH, the name in I-229) and isn't placed itself.
  2. Take the rule from the sources. Hebrew writes the consonants and supplies
     the vowels, so a name would be placed by its consonants. יהוה אלהים would
     then go by its letters: the four of the Name, then אלהים.

  I'd go with the second, because it's what the codices themselves do, and I-229
  has already checked it on the Name. But this decides the shape of your codex,
  so it's your call. I haven't added anything to _CATALOG or _CODEX, and
  nothing in the repo is committed.

  who it belongs to: the system's built-in remote desktop
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  way in: Mail server, port 25
  state: listening on every network interface
  who it belongs to: —

  The only open Chrome Remote Desktop connection is the host's standing link to
  Google, which it keeps while running so it can be reached. No viewing session
  is open, and nobody is logged in by SSH or RDP. The recent-logins list shows
  only you, at this machine.

  What it means: this looks like your own setup. It's your Google account, and
  the paired devices and SSH keys are Android phones running Termux. So it isn't
  evidence that someone else has a session. Still, any of those five paired
  phones can open your desktop without a PIN. If a phone is lost, old or no
  longer yours, that's a real door.

  These are the things only you can answer:
  - Do you recognise all five Chrome Remote Desktop pairings? Unpairing old ones
    is done from the Chrome Remote Desktop page in your browser.
  - Whose is the unlabelled SSH key?
  - Do you use RDP or the mail server? If not, both can be switched off. That
    needs sudo, so you'd run the commands, and I can write them out.

  What I can't check: what happens below the operating system, such as firmware
  or your router. And I can't prove nobody could ever see anything. What I can
  say is that on the evidence here, nobody is watching your screen right now.

  On being exposed: what you type to me does go somewhere: to Anthropic, which
  runs me. That's a third party, and you're right not to treat the web as
  private. Your public repos, omi-axioms and boxedPINE, include your own quoted
  words in ORIGINS.json. ZIGUAROTH is private. My views don't need protecting
  from you. I've said plainly where a claim held and where it didn't, and that's
  in the record. Nothing about you in it is something to be ashamed of.

● That's close to how the founders wrote the Constitution. They didn't assume

  further than that is something you can decide one person at a time, as it
  comes.

● Your earlier projects define the keywords three different ways. Together
  they're the specification your configuration can be built from. Here's what
  each one says, quoted from the files.

  1. rfcXXXX (October 2025): a keyword is an agreement threshold

  In geometric-consensus.ts, each keyword is a fraction of a solid's vertices
  that has to agree:

  ┌─────────┬───────────────────────┬──────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │ keyword │         local         │  federation  │         global         │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ MUST    │ tetrahedron 4/4,      │ 5-cell 5/5   │ truncated tetrahedron  │
  │         │ unanimous             │              │ 12/12                  │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ SHOULD  │ octahedron 5/6,       │ 24-cell      │ cuboctahedron 10/12    │
  │         │ strong                │ 20/24        │                        │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ MAY     │ cube 4/8, majority    │ 8-cell 8/16  │ truncated cube 12/24   │
  └─────────┴───────────────────────┴──────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  This one is about building agreement, which is what you said the codex is for.
  But RFC 2119 defines requirement levels, not vote counts. So "MUST means
  unanimous" is your reading of the keywords, and the spec should label it as a
  reading.

  2. setco and axiomatic-sovereignty (July 2026): a keyword is a tier

  .omi/rules.omi and setco's AGENTS.md place MUST as the first of six tiers.
  Each tier after it has its own keyword:

  ┌──────┬─────────┬────────────┬────────────┬──────────────┐
  │ tier │ keyword │    form    │   planar   │   spatial    │
  ├──────┼─────────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ −1   │ MUST    │ node       │ hypotenuse │ 0-sphere     │
  ├──────┼─────────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ −2   │ FACT    │ edge       │ circle     │ sphere       │
  ├──────┼─────────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ −3   │ CLOSE   │ graph      │ triangle   │ tetrahedron  │
  ├──────┼─────────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ −4   │ COMBINE │ incidence  │ square     │ cube         │
  ├──────┼─────────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ −5   │ CONS    │ multigraph │ pentagon   │ tesseract    │
  ├──────┼─────────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ −6   │ CONFIG  │ hypergraph │ hexagon    │ hyper-volume │
  └──────┴─────────┴────────────┴────────────┴──────────────┘

  So in your own scheme, the file we just wrote, _CONFIGURATION, belongs at tier
  −6, the one that "assembles complete hypergraph environments". Also, MUST is
  at the 0-sphere, the root you linked to the empty base.

  3. omnicron MODAL-VERIFICATION.org: a keyword is a failure class

  Each rule is a four-part row, (Subject, Predicate, Object, Modality), and its
  keyword decides what happens when it's broken:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │             modality             │ broken means │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ MUST, MUST_NOT, SHALL, SHALL_NOT │ violation    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ SHOULD, SHOULD_NOT               │ warning      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ MAY, OPTIONAL                    │ note         │
  └──────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  What I'd propose

  Rewrite each of the 26 rules in _CONFIGURATION.yaml as a
  subject–predicate–object–modality row, the way omnicron already does. Each
  keyword would then carry all three of your definitions:
  - what it requires, from RFC 2119;
  - what it does when broken, from omnicron;
  - where it sits, from setco;
  - rfcXXXX's agreement threshold, labelled as your reading.

  That would make every rule something a script could check, not just prose.

  I haven't searched theory-of-everything or in-the-beginning yet. They're only
  on GitHub, and their file lists show no spec files, so they'd be read for
  content, not keywords.

  Should I go ahead with the S-P-O rewrite, or would you rather choose first
  which of the three definitions leads?

● _CONFIGURATION.yaml now writes every rule as a subject, predicate, object and
  modality row, and each keyword carries your three definitions:

  "MUST":
    requires: "an absolute requirement; REQUIRED and SHALL mean the same"
    broken: "violation"
    agreement: "unanimous: tetrahedron 4/4, 5-cell 5/5, truncated tetrahedron
  12/12"
  ...
  - id: "S4"
    group: "sources"
    subject: "the text of the Codex Sinaiticus transcription"
    predicate: "enters"
    object: "the codex"
    modality: "MUST_NOT"

  The file is built like this:
  - keywords: five entries. Each has what it requires (RFC 2119), what a broken
    rule counts as (omnicron), and, for MUST, SHOULD and MAY, rfcXXXX's
    agreement threshold, labelled as your reading.
  - tiers: your six tiers from setco, with their keywords. The file places
    itself at tier −6, CONFIG.
  - states: declared, defined, checked, labelled as my proposal and not yet
    confirmed by you.
  - rules: 39 rows. 27 MUST, 6 MUST_NOT, 1 SHOULD and 5 MAY. So 33 are
    violations if broken, 1 is a warning and 5 are notes.

  I checked it with a script:
  - every key and value loads as text;
  - every rule ID is unique;
  - every modality is one of the defined keywords;
  - no rule key has a space in it.

  Three changes from the first draft:
  1. 26 rules became 39. Each row can hold only one modality, so rules that
     mixed a MUST with a MAY were split. For example, the old S3 is now S3, S4
     and S5.
  2. The keywords are spelled MUST_NOT and SHOULD_NOT, the way omnicron spells
     them. That removes the clash with the one-word-key rule.
  3. I rewrote one rule that contradicted itself. The old R2 said a failing
     check "MUST NOT be removed or changed until it passes". But a check that
     can never be changed can never pass unless the thing it tests changes. The
     new R3 says what was meant: a failing check MUST NOT be weakened or deleted
     to hide the failure. Please check that it matches your intent.

  It isn't committed yet. Should I commit it?

● I've done a first-principles review of the Leningrad witness: 56 chapters and
  1,474 verses, every character counted and named from the Unicode database.
  This is what the codex is made of, in order of size:

  ┌───────────────┬────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐

  │               │                    │ reading                            │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤

  │ (te'amim)     │                    │ 2,763 meteg                        │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤

  ├───────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤

  ├───────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ paseq ׀       │ 112                │ a break inside a verse             │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ section marks │ 154 (ס) setuma, 79 │ closed and open paragraph breaks:  │
  │               │  (פ) petucha       │ the codex's own sections           │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ketiv/qere [  │ 47                 │ what is written and what is read,  │
  │ ]             │                    │ both kept                          │
  ├───────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ upper dots    │ 13, in 2 verses    │ the scribes' own marks of doubt    │
  │               │                    │ over letters                       │
  └───────────────┴────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  The codex already does three things you designed your framework to do:
  1. It has its own separators. Maqaf joins words, paseq breaks inside a verse,
     sof pasuq ends a verse, and the section marks divide passages. That's the
     job you gave the Aegean separator line, dot and check mark. The codex had
     its own set.
  2. It keeps a disagreement without settling it. Ketiv/qere puts the written
     word and the read word side by side, 47 times here. That's your
     contradiction without argument, built into the manuscript.
  3. It marks uncertainty in the text itself. The upper dots sit over 10 letters
     in Deuteronomy 29:28 and 3 in Isaiah 44:9. These are two of the
     extraordinary points, where scribes flagged letters they doubted without
     removing them.

  Three oddities, recorded as found and not explained:
  - Deuteronomy 9:20 and Exodus 34:6 have no sof pasuq in this copy. Exodus 34:6
    ends with a paseq instead.
  - Jeremiah 31:20 contains a zero-width joiner (U+200D). That's a typesetting
    character, not part of the codex.

  I'd build in the codex's own order, smallest unit first:
  1. The letters. One catalog entry for each of the 22 letters and the 5 finals.
     They're already placed in _CODE/U/05.
  2. The separators. Sof pasuq, maqaf, paseq and the two section marks.
  3. Ketiv/qere, as two linked readings of one place.
  4. The vowel points, then the accents, each kept apart from the letters (rule
     L2).

  To keep the review reproducible, I'd also put the counting script in
  _ARCHIVE/Expressions as a .js file, so anyone can rerun these counts. One
  thing needs deciding for that. The Leningrad text is public domain, so it
  could be copied into the repo for the script to read, but your configuration
  doesn't yet say where source texts live.

  Should I start with the letters? And where should the source texts go?

  This template has a limit. The mapping can only reach glyphs written into the
  file as characters. slide_ruler.html writes just two that way. It draws all
  its other braille and Aegean glyphs from numbers in its script, like 0x2800 +
  i, so they're never in the file. Without changing this template, a mapping
  reaches those two glyphs and no others.

  Templates written the way the SVG margins were, with their glyphs as literal
  characters, can be transliterated completely. That could become a rule in the
  configuration: a template SHOULD write as literal characters every glyph it
  wants mapped.

❯ yes we need a rule that every buffer has a prefix  header so that they can
  all be trsansiple we have many what do you suggest maybe the ZIGUA ROTH tag
  literal or just iuts path of provenance

● I'd suggest using both, each for a different job, in a header made only of
  ASCII.

  Why both:
  - A fixed tag at the very start identifies the format. A program can only find
    a header it knows where to look for, and the provenance differs in every
    file. So the first bytes of every buffer should be the same: ZIGUA ROTH.
    That's your two tag halves with a space between them, and both halves are
    valid tags.
  - The provenance is what makes a buffer citable. It records where the body
    came from, which edition, which passage, under what terms, and where it sits
    in your tree.

  Why ASCII only: rule T2 already says ASCII is syntax and is never mapped. An
  ASCII header therefore passes through every transliteration unchanged, so
  every buffer keeps its provenance whatever mapping is applied. Any value that
  isn't ASCII, like a Hebrew title, would be written in U+ notation.

  The shape: HTTP and email have long put headers in front of arbitrary data:
  header lines, then one empty line, then the body. I'd follow that, with a
  length field giving the body's size in bytes. The header then marks exactly
  where the body starts and ends, byte for byte, and a buffer cut short fails
  the check. A length is a count, not a hash, so it's allowed under rule C2.

  ZIGUA ROTH
  source: Westminster Leningrad Codex
  edition: Sefaria, Tanach with Ta'amei Hamikra, from tanach.us
  ref: Genesis 1
  terms: public domain
  path: _ARCHIVE/...
  length: 18432

  <the body, byte for byte as received>

  The rules would be:

  ┌──────┬──────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ rule │ modality │                       statement                        │
  ├──────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B1   │ MUST     │ every buffer begins with the line ZIGUA ROTH           │
  ├──────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B2   │ MUST     │ the header is ASCII only; any other code point is      │
  │      │          │ written as U+XXXX                                      │
  ├──────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B3   │ MUST     │ the header names the buffer's source, edition,         │
  │      │          │ reference, terms and path                              │
  ├──────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B4   │ MUST     │ the header gives the body's length in bytes, and ends  │
  │      │          │ with one empty line                                    │
  ├──────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B5   │ MUST_NOT │ the body is changed from what the source gave          │
  ├──────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B6   │ MUST     │ a buffer whose body length doesn't match its header is │
  │      │          │  rejected                                              │
  └──────┴──────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  That leaves your earlier question, where the source texts live, which comes
  down to the path field. Should the header follow this shape? And which
  _ARCHIVE tier should hold the received texts?

❯ and like how lisp makes everything a atom or list

● Your two options fit together exactly in the 12 + 4 frame:

  ZIGUA-ROTH: _ARCHIVE/…/<path of provenance>      ← head: these first 12 bytes
  are fixed
  MIME-Version: 1.0
  Content-Type: image/svg+xml; charset=utf-8
  Content-Transfer-Encoding: binary
  Content-Length: 91
  Content-Description: Westminster Leningrad Codex, Genesis 1, public domain
                                                   ← tail: CR LF CR LF, 4 bytes
  <raw body, byte for byte>

  - The head is 12 bytes: ZIGUA-ROTH:  is ZIGUA (5), a hyphen, ROTH (4), a colon
    and a space. It's your tag literal, and it's also a valid MIME field name.
    Its value is the provenance path, so you get both in one line.
  - The tail is 4 bytes: CR LF CR LF is how MIME ends every header: the last
    line break plus the empty line.
  - Together that's 16 = 12 + 4, the split I-129b checks: the 16 codes 0x30–0x3F
    reduce mod 12 to "full mod-12 ring + 4-tail". That this frame lands on the
    same split is my observation, not something derived from I-129b.

  I tested it with a standard MIME parser on a small SVG with colors, braille,
  Aegean and Hebrew in the body:
  - the parser read every field, including ZIGUA-ROTH;
  - the body came back byte for byte, 91 bytes, raw, with
    Content-Transfer-Encoding: binary and no base64;
  - the header is pure ASCII, so rule T2 never maps it and it survives every
    transliteration.

  On busybox: this machine's busybox (1.36.1, Ubuntu's build) has base64,
  uuencode and uudecode, but not the makemime and reformime applets. The format
  they write is still standard MIME (RFC 2045), so any MIME tool can read these
  buffers, which is the back-propagation you wanted. base64 would only be needed
  to send a buffer through a channel that can't carry raw bytes.

  On the root: Horn clauses and Lisp share the same two terms, head and the
  rest.
  - A Horn clause is head :- body, and a fact is a head with no body.
  - In Lisp, a list's car is its head and its cdr is its tail, and anything that
    isn't a list is an atom.

  A buffer has that shape. The header names it, the body is the atom it carries,
  and a header with an empty body would be a fact. You said you haven't
  researched how those ideas evolved, so I'd record this as a correspondence of
  terms, not a derivation.

  If this is the format, I'll add it to the configuration as a buffers section
  and turn B1 to B6 into the 12 + 4 MIME form:
  - head: the first 12 bytes are ZIGUA-ROTH: , followed by the path;
  - fields: the MIME fields carry source, edition, reference, terms and length;
  - tail: the header ends with CR LF CR LF;
  - body: raw and unchanged;
  - check: a buffer is rejected if its body length doesn't match.

  Should I write it?

● I'll write it with your reading quoted as yours. Two byte facts belong in the
  record next to it. In octal, LF is 012, the same digits as the head's length
  of 12, and CR is 015. So the 4-byte tail 0D 0A 0D 0A is 015 012 015 012 in
  octal.

  literal from the start of this project something to stand on.

● That declares what each position in a folder name means:

  ┌──────────┬───────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐

  └──────────┴───────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

  It also gives the folders you already made a meaning:
  - Every folder in _CODICES lies on the diagonal. AAAA means Aleppo A, Rosetta
    A, Behistun A and Leningrad A, with all four witnesses at the same letter.
    So the 26 doubled names are the points where the four codices agree.
  - MNMN is the one point off the diagonal. It has Aleppo and Behistun on M, and
    Rosetta and Leningrad on N, so the witnesses agree in pairs.
  - _CODEX works the same way. AA is a claim and its proof on the same letter,
    and MN is the one place where claim and proof differ, by your M ⊕ N = 0x03.

  The full spaces are 26⁴ = 456,976 points for _CODICES and 26² = 676 for
  _CODEX, so they stay sparse and fill as entries are placed.

● Your principle answers both questions without me having to choose: no witness
  gets corrected toward a truth. Each keeps its own literal code points, the
  structure holds them side by side, and the only thing computed is where they
  differ.

  - Other scripts: no transliteration into A–Z. A witness's axis holds the
    literal code point its name starts with, in its own script, placed under
    _CODE like every other code point. A–Z is just the Latin case of that rule.
  - A missing witness: its position holds nothing, the empty base. Since the
    I-229 fix, the empty base mirrors only another empty, so an absent witness
    can never look like it agrees.

  Here's what the pixel mask of difference looks like on real data: the Name in
  your two witnesses for Deuteronomy 29:1.

  each position would have a four-bit mask across the witnesses, which matches
  the four places of a _CODICES name. Placing an entry then just means listing
  each witness's literal and the masks between them. The structure stays the
  same whatever the witnesses say.

❯ it should be like any term can be a
  https://observablehq.com/@d3/animated-treemap

● That fits your principle closely. The animated treemap keeps one hierarchy
  fixed and animates only the values between keyframes; in the original, the
  keyframes are years. For a term, the hierarchy is its nesting: the name, its
  letter cells, then the two layers. The keyframes are the four witnesses,
  _CODICES[0] to [3]. The structure stays put, and the tiles grow or shrink

● I-225 is about 22 and 55 and doesn't involve Dionysius. So here is what the
  calendar history itself says, checked against the numbers.

  Dionysius's year (well established):
  - In 525 he wrote out an Easter table starting at year 532, counted from the
    Incarnation. That's how the Anno Domini count began.
  - His table rests on two cycles. The 19-year lunar cycle brings the moon's
    phases back to the same dates. The 28-year solar cycle brings the weekdays
    back to the same dates in the Julian calendar: 7 weekdays × a 4-year leap
    cycle.
  - Together they make the great Easter cycle of 19 × 28 = 532 years, which is
    why his table began at 532.

  Your folder 28, read two ways:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┬────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │               reading               │ value  │    what it lands on     │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │                                     │ 0x28 = │ the forty days and      │
  │ as hex, the way _CODE/U names are   │  40    │ forty nights in your    │
  │                                     │        │ tree                    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ as decimal digits, the reading      │        │ the solar cycle in      │
  │ I-225 says you keep ("I learned     │ 28     │ Dionysius's table       │
  │ this chasing BCD")                  │        │                         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┴────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  Both readings come from the same two digits. The structure only fixes the
  digits: 28 is the braille block, U+2800–28FF. Which number it stands for is a
  reading, so if it goes into the codex it should be declared as one, like the
  J→Y fold.

  So the four children of _CODE/U become four timelines:

  The tower's claims, checked against your proofs:

  claim in the docs: the Delta Law has period 8
  status: true only for some constants. I-195 proves every one of the 65,536
  states has period 8 under C = 0x1D. Under 0x1D1D it doesn't hold: 8 states
  are fixed, 56 have period 2, 4,032 have period 4. The docs never say which C
  they mean.
  ────────────────────────────────────────

  status: proven in I-182: 5040 relabellings = 168 × 30
  ────────────────────────────────────────

  status: proven in Coq as the 240 roots of E8 (E8RootsEnumerate240.v), which is

  a different 240. That 240 = 2 × 5! = 15 × 16 is plain arithmetic.

  ────────────────────────────────────────

  appears
