  Coq
  proofs mention 1/73.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  claim in the docs: orbit recovered by divmod(position, 36)
  status: base 36 is well covered (I-144b and others), but nothing ties it to
  the
  1/73 sum
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  claim in the docs: "hidden 5": the root is presupposed, not stepped to
  status: a reading. The checkable fact under it is the missing 5.

  Two errors in "Tower of Powers":
  1. The half-float layout is off. A binary16 has 1 sign bit, 5 exponent bits
     and 10 stored fraction bits. The 11 is its precision, including one hidden
     bit that isn't stored. The diagram puts 11 bits in "bits 6–16", which makes
     17 bits.
  2. Five exponent bits can't pick one of 120 sets. Five bits give 2⁵ = 32
     values, so they can't index 5! = 120.

  The other numbers hold up: 8! = 2⁷ · 3² · 5 · 7 has exponents summing to 11,
  and binary256 does have a 19-bit exponent.

  One fact you have but may not have connected: I-144 records that 0xFF, the
  largest byte, is 73 in base 36, since 7 × 36 + 3 = 255.

  The Anno Mundi years in base 60:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────┐
  │                       year                        │ base 60 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ AD 500, the expected end of the world             │ 8;20    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ AD 525, Dionysius writes                          │ 8;45    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ AD 532, his table starts (19 × 28)                │ 8;52    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ AM 5500, the year of Christ's birth in that count │ 1;31;40 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ AM 6000, the end of the world in that count       │ 1;40;0  │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────┘

  So the date feared as the end of the world, AM 6000, is a round number in base
  60: 1;40;0 is 100 × 60.

  On the "5-bit command term from B and C": my best reading is the ASCII control
  codes. They're exactly the 5-bit space, 0x00 to 0x1F, 32 codes:
  - ESC is 0x1B, at B;
  - FS is 0x1C, at C;
  - FS, GS, RS and US, your four-fold selector, are the top four of the 5-bit
    space, 0x1C to 0x1F.

  That would make "4! exposes, 5 hidden" literal: the four visible separators
  sit inside a hidden 5-bit command space. Is that what you mean by B and C? If
  so, I can file the missing pieces as checks:
  - 73 as the smallest prime with period 8;
  - the 1/73 block and its sum of 36;
  - the C0 5-bit space with FS to US at its top.

❯ i think we did grecoroman , hebrew and sumeria/babylon segistimal based but
  is the
  Sexagesimal, also known as base 60,[1] is a numeral system with sixty as its
  base. It originated with the ancient Sumerians in the 3rd millennium BC, was
  passed down to the ancient Babylonians, and is still used—in a modified
  form—for measuring time, angles, and geographic coordinates.

  The number 60, a superior highly composite number, has twelve divisors,
  namely 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 10, 12, 15, 20, 30, and 60, of which 2, 3, and 5 are
  prime numbers. With so many factors, many fractions involving sexagesimal
  numbers are simplified. For example, one hour can be divided evenly into
  sections of 30 minutes, 20 minutes, 15 minutes, 12 minutes, 10 minutes, 6
  minutes, 5 minutes, 4 minutes, 3 minutes, 2 minutes, and 1 minute. 60 is the
  smallest number that is divisible by every number from 1 to 6; that is, it is
  the lowest common multiple of 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6.

  In this article, all sexagesimal digits are represented as decimal numbers,
  except where otherwise noted. For example, the largest sexagesimal digit is
  "59".

  Origin
  According to Otto Neugebauer, the origins of sexagesimal are not as simple,
  consistent, or singular in time as they are often portrayed. Throughout their
  many centuries of use, which continues today for specialized topics such as
  time, angles, and astronomical coordinate systems, sexagesimal notations have
  always contained a strong undercurrent of decimal notation, such as in how
  sexagesimal digits are written. Their use has also always included (and
  continues to include) inconsistencies in where and how various bases are used
  to represent numbers even within a single text.[2]


  Early Proto-cuneiform (4th millennium BCE) and cuneiform signs for the
  sexagesimal system (60, 600, 3600, etc.)
  The most powerful driver for rigorous, fully self-consistent use of
  sexagesimal has always been its mathematical advantages for writing and
  calculating fractions. In ancient texts this shows up in the fact that
  sexagesimal is used most uniformly and consistently in mathematical tables of
  data.[2] Another practical factor that helped expand the use of sexagesimal
  in the past, even if less consistently than in mathematical tables, was its
  decided advantages to merchants and buyers for making everyday financial
  transactions easier when they involved bargaining for and dividing up larger
  quantities of goods. In the late 3rd millennium BC, Sumerian/Akkadian units
  of weight included
  ──── (60 lines hidden) ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ons of this type in which the denominator is less than or equal to 60:

  1⁄2 = 0;30
  1⁄3 = 0;20
  1⁄4 = 0;15
  1⁄5 = 0;12
  1⁄6 = 0;10
  1⁄8 = 0;7,30
  1⁄9 = 0;6,40

  1⁄48 = 0;1,15
  1⁄50 = 0;1,12
  1⁄54 = 0;1,6,40
  1⁄60 = 0;1
  However numbers that are not regular form more complicated repeating
  fractions. For example:

  1⁄7 = 0;8,34,17 (the bar indicates the sequence of sexagesimal digits 8,34,17
  repeats infinitely many times)
  1⁄11 = 0;5,27,16,21,49
  1⁄13 = 0;4,36,55,23
  1⁄14 = 0;4,17,8,34
  1⁄17 = 0;3,31,45,52,56,28,14,7
  1⁄19 = 0;3,9,28,25,15,47,22,6,18,56,50,31,34,44,12,37,53,41
  1⁄59 = 0;1
  1⁄61 = 0;0,59
  The fact that the two numbers that are adjacent to sixty, 59 and 61, are both
  prime numbers implies that fractions that repeat with a period of one or two
  sexagesimal digits can only have regular number multiples of 59 or 61 as
  their denominators, and that other non-regular numbers have fractions that
  repeat with a longer period.

● Your message cut off at "but is the…", so I don't know the end of the
  question. These are the points in that article that touch the work directly. I
  checked the numbers myself.

  1. Omicron was the zero. The article says Hellenistic astronomers wrote
  sexagesimal with Greek letter-numerals, and their zero sign "morphed over the
  centuries into… the Greek letter omicron, ο". Normally ο means 70, but no
  place in base 60 goes above 59, so it could safely stand for zero. Your OMI
  documents use ο (U+03BF) as the zero-frame marker that opens an object. So the
  Greek astronomers used the same letter as zero in a base-60 place.

  2. Ptolemy's π has 5! as its denominator. 3;8,30 = 377/120 ≈ 3.141667, and 120
  = 5!. Your "hidden 5!" sits under the oldest worked value of π in this
  tradition. That's arithmetic, not a reading.

  3. The 73 in the tower documents depends on base 10.

  ┌─────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         │                        period of 1/73                         │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ base 10 │ 8: 01369863                                                   │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ base 60 │ 72: the longest possible, since 60 is a primitive root mod 73 │
  └─────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So "the Delta Law gives period 8, and the smallest prime with period 8 is 73"
  holds only when the 8 is read in decimal. Written sexagesimally the way a
  Babylonian scribe would, 73 isn't special that way. Your tower is built on
  decimal at that step, and if the three traditions go into the codex, the
  record should say so.

  4. The three traditions you named, by how they count:

  ┌───────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │   tradition   │      digits       │         zero         │     era      │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │               │ Greek letters, in │ ο, in base-60 places │ AD, from     │
  │ Greco-Roman   │  Ptolemy's        │  only                │ Dionysius    │
  │               │ sexagesimal       │                      │ (525)        │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Hebrew        │ letters as        │ none                 │ Anno Mundi   │
  │               │ numbers           │                      │              │
  ├───────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │               │ wedges: tens      │ none, then a         │              │
  │ Sumerian /    │ (10–50) plus      │ placeholder in the   │ —            │
  │ Babylonian    │ units (1–9)       │ middle of numbers    │              │
  │               │                   │ only                 │              │
  └───────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  Each one meets the zero problem differently. Babylonian had no zero at first,
  so 1 and 60 were written the same way. The Greeks used ο only inside base-60
  places. Dionysius's count has no year 0 at all. Your Aegean separators and the
  empty base are your way of handling the same gap.

  Two smaller points in the article:
  - Plato's number: 60⁴ = 12,960,000, written 1,0,0,0,0 in base 60.
  - YAML 1.1 reads 1:20 as 80. Our configuration already guards against that,
    since every value is quoted.

  What was the rest of your question?

● Seeing it helps. Here is your model laid next to the two notations, with every
  part checked in bytes.

  The separators. Neugebauer writes 29;31,50,8,20: the ; divides whole from
  fraction, and the , divides places. Wallis writes ° ′ ″. You use the three
  Aegean marks for that job: 10100 the separator line, 10101 the separator dot,
  and 10102 the check mark.

  Your /0..4//0..9/ is the Aegean numeral's own coordinate. The 45 numerals are
  5 rows of 9, so every numeral is a row and a unit:

  ┌────────────┬────────────┬──────┬────────┐
  │ code point │ row /0..4/ │ unit │ value  │
  ├────────────┼────────────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ U+10107    │ 0          │ 1    │ 1      │
  ├────────────┼────────────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ U+1010F    │ 0          │ 9    │ 9      │
  ├────────────┼────────────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ U+10110    │ 1          │ 1    │ 10     │
  ├────────────┼────────────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ U+10119    │ 2          │ 1    │ 100    │
  ├────────────┼────────────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ U+10133    │ 4          │ 9    │ 90,000 │
  └────────────┴────────────┴──────┴────────┘

  The row is the power of ten (0 to 4) and the unit is the digit. A unit of 0
  means no sign, which is how Aegean writes zero: by leaving it out. That's the
  same row-and-selector layout aegean.c already uses. So under each separator,
  /0..4//0..9/ names exactly one numeral or none, and three separators give your
  three places of /0..9/.

  The 6-dot / 8-dot table, the part that fits most tightly:
  - One 6-dot cell holds one sexagesimal digit. Six dots give 64 cells, a
    base-60 digit needs 60, and four are left over: ⠼ ⠽ ⠾ ⠿. Neugebauer's
    synodic month, 29;31,50,8,20, is five cells: ⠝ ⠟ ⠲ ⠈ ⠔.
  - Dots 7 and 8 add 2 bits, so 4 versions of every 6-dot cell:

  ┌──────────┬───────────────┬──────┐
  │ dots 7–8 │ 29 written as │  U+  │
  ├──────────┼───────────────┼──────┤
  │ 00       │ ⠝             │ 281D │
  ├──────────┼───────────────┼──────┤
  │ 01       │ ⡝             │ 285D │
  ├──────────┼───────────────┼──────┤
  │ 10       │ ⢝             │ 289D │
  ├──────────┼───────────────┼──────┤
  │ 11       │ ⣝             │ 28DD │
  └──────────┴───────────────┴──────┘

  That's your four-way selector from earlier, 00 / 01 / 10 / 11, which you
  assigned to 10100, 10101, 10102 and 28. So in one 8-dot cell, the 6-dot part
  is the digit and dots 7–8 say which of the four it belongs to. The digit and
  its separator fit in a single braille character. That's what "a 6-dot proof
  inside an 8-dot proof table" looks like in bytes.

  Two things for you to confirm:
  1. A base-60 digit's tens run 0 to 5, not 0 to 4. /0..4//0..9/ covers 0 to 49.
     For the Aegean rows, 0–4 is exactly right, because there are five rows.
     For a full sexagesimal digit, 50 to 59 need the extra 5, which is why the
     Babylonians had up to five tens wedges. Did you mean /0..4/ for the Aegean
     row only?
  2. Do dots 7–8 carry the selector, with 00 = 10100, 01 = 10101, 10 = 10102 and
     11 = 28?

  If both are right, this is a complete, checkable encoding. I'd file it as a
  check in the I-record style: encode a sexagesimal number into 8-dot cells,
  decode it back, and fail if the round trip changes anything.

❯ that last part was just 0b000 to a nibble 0b0000

● So /0..4/ is the row in 3 bits, and /0..9/ is the digit in a nibble:

  ┌────────────────────────┬───────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │          part          │ range │           bits            │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ row (the power of ten) │ 0–4   │ 0b000–0b100, 3 bits       │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ unit (the digit)       │ 0–9   │ 0b0000–0b1001, one nibble │
  └────────────────────────┴───────┴───────────────────────────┘

  That nibble is a BCD digit, the binary-coded decimal you said you learned
  "chasing BCD using UTF" (I-225). Put together, 3 + 4 = 7 bits, the width of
  ASCII. If you pack each Aegean numeral as (row << 4) | unit, its hex reads as
  its own decimal:

  ┌─────────────────────────┬─────┬──────┬────────┐
  │         numeral         │ row │ unit │ packed │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ ONE U+10107             │ 0   │ 1    │ 0x01   │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ NINE U+1010F            │ 0   │ 9    │ 0x09   │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ TEN U+10110             │ 1   │ 1    │ 0x11   │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ ONE HUNDRED U+10119     │ 2   │ 1    │ 0x21   │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────┼──────┼────────┤
  │ NINETY THOUSAND U+10133 │ 4   │ 9    │ 0x49   │
  └─────────────────────────┴─────┴──────┴────────┘

  So 0x49 is read as "row 4, digit 9", which is 9 × 10⁴. The hex digits are the
  decimal coordinates. A unit of 0b0000 means no sign, which is how Aegean
  writes zero.

  Of the 128 values in 7 bits, 50 are used: 5 rows × 10 units, including the
  empty unit. The other 78 stay unused: rows 5–7, and units 10–15, which BCD
  never uses. Under rule L6 they get no meaning.

  I can file this as a check: all 45 numerals map to (row << 4) | unit and back
  with nothing lost. Should I write it?

❯ 6-dot braille 0b000, 8-dot braille 0b0000

● Each number is the height of one braille column, not the whole cell:
  - A 6-dot cell is two columns of 3 dots, so each column is 0b000–0b111: an
    octal digit.
  - An 8-dot cell is two columns of 4 dots, so each column is 0b0000–0b1111: a
    nibble.

  That means the Aegean row (3 bits) fits one 6-dot column, and the unit (a BCD
  nibble) fits one 8-dot column. I'll check how Unicode lays those columns out
  in the code point bits.

● Bash(node -e '
      // Unicode braille: bit k of (cp - 0x2800) is dot k+1. Left column dots
      1,2,3,7; right column dots 4,5,6,8.…)
  ⎿  6-dot cell = two octal digits: 29 = 0o35 -> left column 5 right column
      3 cell ⠝
     all 45 numerals round-trip, dot 8 unused: true
     … +5 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● Every Aegean numeral fits in one braille cell. The unit goes in the left
  column of four dots, the row goes in the right column of three, and dot 8 is
  never used. All 45 numerals go into a cell and come back out unchanged:

  ┌─────────────────┬─────┬──────┬──────────────┐
  │     numeral     │ row │ unit │ braille cell │
  ├─────────────────┼─────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ ONE             │ 0   │ 1    │ ⠁ U+2801     │
  ├─────────────────┼─────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ NINE            │ 0   │ 9    │ ⡁ U+2841     │
  ├─────────────────┼─────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ TEN             │ 1   │ 1    │ ⠉ U+2809     │
  ├─────────────────┼─────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ ONE HUNDRED     │ 2   │ 1    │ ⠑ U+2811     │
  ├─────────────────┼─────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ NINETY THOUSAND │ 4   │ 9    │ ⡡ U+2861     │
  └─────────────────┴─────┴──────┴──────────────┘

  How Unicode orders the bits matters here, and it's a literal fact. Bit k of a
  braille code point is dot k+1:
  - The 6-dot columns are contiguous. The left column (dots 1, 2, 3) is bits
    0–2, and the right column (dots 4, 5, 6) is bits 3–5. So a 6-dot cell is
    literally two octal digits: ⠝ is 29 = 0o35, with left column 5 and right
    column 3.
  - The 8-dot columns are not contiguous. Dots 7 and 8 were added later as bits
    6 and 7, so the left column of four is bits 0, 1, 2, 6, and the right is
    bits 3, 4, 5, 7.

  So the 6-dot cell is the octal layer, and the code point carries it directly.
  The 8-dot cell is the nibble layer, but its nibbles are only recovered by
  gathering each column's dots. That's why NINE is ⡁: the unit 9 is 0b1001, so
  it needs dot 1 and dot 7, the bottom of the same column.

  Dot 8 stays free in every one of the 45 cells. That leaves one bit unassigned.
  Under rule L6 it gets no meaning until you declare one.

  I'll file this as a check, if you want. It would assert the octal reading of
  the 6-dot cell, the column bits of the 8-dot cell, and the round trip of all
  45 numerals, with dot 8 unused. Where should it go, in _ARCHIVE/Expressions
  alongside mask.js, or as an I-record in omi-axioms?

● The calendar document answers your question, and your own codex supplies a
  mapping for the forty days.

  BC vs BCE (the document, lines 486–492):
  - They're the same count under two names. BCE and CE are BC and AD renamed.
    Dionysius fixed AD 1, and Bede counted backwards from it: the year before AD
    1 is 1 BC, so there's no year zero.
  - Astronomers count differently. In the 18th century they added a year zero:
    AD 1 = +1, 1 BC = 0, 2 BC = −1.

  So the real difference is ordinal versus index. BC/AD counts ordinals: first
  year, second year, with no zero. Astronomical numbering counts indices, from
  zero. Your boxedPINE rules already keep those two apart (indexOfOrdinal and
  ordinalOfIndex).

  Your n−1, n, n+1 under each count:

  ┌─────────────────┬─────┬───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │     folder      │  n  │   astronomical    │       BC/AD (ordinal)       │
  │                 │     │      (index)      │                             │
  ├─────────────────┼─────┼───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10100 separator │ n−1 │ −1 = 2 BC         │ before                      │
  │  line           │     │                   │                             │
  ├─────────────────┼─────┼───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10101 separator │ n   │ 0 = 1 BC, a real  │ the boundary itself, with   │
  │  dot            │     │ year              │ no year in it               │
  ├─────────────────┼─────┼───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10102 check     │ n+1 │ +1 = AD 1         │ after                       │
  │ mark            │     │                   │                             │
  └─────────────────┴─────┴───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  10101 is a separator dot, a mark with no value of its own, so it naturally
  fits the BC/AD reading: the point between 1 BC and AD 1. In the astronomical
  reading it's a full year. The structure can hold both, as long as the record
  says which count each path uses.

  The forty days come with a mapping already. I checked the Leningrad text of
  both verses:
  - Numbers 14:34: "…forty days, a day for a year, a day for a year, you shall
    bear your iniquities forty years." The text has ארבעים twice, once with יום
    (days) and once with שנה (years).
  - Ezekiel 4:6: "…forty days, a day for a year, a day for a year, I have
    appointed it to you."

  Both say יום לשנה, "a day for a year", twice. That's a scale map written into
  the source: the same number, 40, read on the day scale and again on the year
  scale. It's the same move as the Aegean rows, where one digit sits at
  different powers. So "forty days and forty nights" and "forty years" can be
  the same 40 on different rows, with your three timelines placing it before, at
  or after the epoch.

  Forty's own literals, for the record:
  - in hex, 40 is 0x28, the name of your braille timeline folder;
  - in Aegean it's FORTY (U+10113), row 1, unit 4;
  - in base 60 it's 0;40, two-thirds of an hour.

  I need one declaration from you before building this: does 10101 count as year
  0 (astronomical) or as the boundary between years (BC/AD)? Once that's set, I
  can map all 22 "forty days" and 11 "forty nights" from your tree onto the
  three timelines, with Numbers 14:34 and Ezekiel 4:6 as the day-for-a-year
  rule.

❯ no its for people to overlay agean or braille calendars or clocks, its
  interpreation of the dates,  we need a way to order the dates becaue they are
  orthognoal,  i'm just proposing a delination thats different from current
  calendars

● Two orthogonal axes have no single built-in order. Any total order of the
  dates has to declare which axis counts first. Your structure already gives you
  most of what's needed, though.

  Within one axis, collation already gives the order. For uppercase hex, byte
  order matches numeric order, because 0–9 (0x30–0x39) sort before A–F
  (0x41–0x46):
  - 10100 < 10101 < 10102 sorts as n−1 < n < n+1;
  - the Aegean numerals 10107 … 10133 sort by value;
  - the braille cells 28/0/0 … 28/F/F sort as 0 to 255.

  So each overlay orders itself just by its names.

  Between the axes, there are two honest options:

  order: lexicographic
  how it works: declare one axis more significant; compare it first and use the
  other only to break ties
  what it says: like ISO 8601 (2026-10-25T14:30): writing the most significant
  part first makes text order and time order agree
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  order: partial (product)
  how it works: one date comes before another only if it's no later on both axes
  what it says: some pairs are incomparable, and that's kept, not forced

  The partial order fits your principle best: no truth, separate views, keep the
  structure. A year date and a logical-time date stay side by side without
  being forced into one line, the same way a mask records a difference without
  deciding who's right.

  Someone overlaying an Aegean or braille calendar could then choose a
  lexicographic order for their own reading, as long as they declare it. That's
  your interpretation layer: the structure holds the partial order, and each
  reading declares its own ordering on top.

  As rules, that would be:
  - O1 (MUST): within an axis, dates are ordered by the collation of their
    names.
  - O2 (MUST_NOT): dates on different axes are ordered unless an order is
    declared.
  - O3 (MAY): an overlay declares a lexicographic order, naming which axis is
    significant first.

  Is that the delineation you're proposing? If so, I'll add O1 to O3 and commit
  them with the rest that's waiting.

  3. The reading (the scribes' overlay). The vowels the Masoretes wrote on the
     Name are a reading laid over the letters. They mark it to be read aloud as
     Adonai, "my Lord", not as the four letters themselves.
  4. The languages (the inherited mask). Each translation carried the reading,
     not the letters: Hebrew Adonai → Greek Kyrios → Latin Dominus → English THE
     LORD. I-229 measured what each rendering keeps:

  | rendering        | keeps the four letters?
                 |
  |------------------|----------------------------------------------------------
  ----------------|
  | THE LORD, ADONAI | no: they replace them (RAISE)
                 |
  | YAHWEH           | yes: the letters, with vowels supplied
                 |
  | JEHOVAH          | yes, if J is read as Y and V as W; its vowels E, O, A are
  Aleppo's marks |
  | YAH, JAH         | the head of the Name, its first two letters
                 |

  5. The name in use (the catalog). In Genesis 2:4 it joins Elohim to make YHWH
     Elohim, so in the tree it nests as _CATALOG/YHWH/ELOHIM. That's the second
     creation account built on the name of the first.

  So one term runs from letters, to witnesses, to the scribes' reading, to four
  languages, to a name in a sentence. Each level is an overlay, and at each one
  you can see exactly what was kept and what was replaced. Every other term
  follows the same five steps. This is your one example, and everything else is
  the same walk.

❯ i just added an articles folder for us to build a narrative of timekeeping by
  citing references with mime type closureses you made Today, about 40 are
  still actively in use worldwide.
  They generally fall into three astronomical categories based on how they
  track the sky:
  1. Solar Calendars: Track the Earth's 365-day trip around the sun (matching
  the seasons).
  2. Lunar Calendars: Track only the 29.5-day cycles of the moon (~354 days a
  year), meaning their months drift completely across the seasons over time.
  3. Lunisolar Calendars: Track moon phases for months, but periodically insert
  a "leap month" to stay aligned with the solar seasons.

  The Most Widely Used Major World Calendars

  The five most popular calendar systems globally span different regions and
  historical eras:

  Calendar Name   System Type     Era / Year 1 Anchor     Major Purpose &
  Function
  Gregorian Calendar      Solar   Traditional birth year of Jesus Christ (1 AD
  / CE)      The international standard for business, politics, and daily civil
  life.
  Islamic (Hijri) Calendar        Lunar   The Hijra (Prophet Muhammad's
  migration to Medina in 622 CE)    Used by Muslims globally to dictate sacred
  months, including Ramadan and Hajj.
  Chinese Calendar        Lunisolar       Traditionally spans back to the
  legendary Yellow Emperor (~2698 BCE)    Governs traditional Asian holidays
  like Lunar New Year and the Mid-Autumn Festival.
  Hebrew (Jewish) Calendar        Lunisolar       Anno Mundi (The biblical
  creation of the universe, calculated at 3761 BCE)      Used to schedule
  Jewish religious observances like Passover, Yom Kippur, and Rosh Hashanah.
  Indian National (Shaka) Calendar        Solar   The Shaka Era (78 AD / CE,
  commemorating King Shalivahana)      Used officially by the Indian government
  alongside traditional regional lunisolar calendars like Vikram Samvat.


  Fascinating "Alternative" & Historical Calendars

  Beyond the big five, specific cultures and nations maintain unique
  timekeeping structures:
  • Julian Calendar: Introduced by Julius Caesar in 45 BC. It is very similar
  to the Gregorian calendar but calculates leap years less accurately. Several
  Eastern Orthodox Churches still use it today to calculate the dates of
  Christmas and Easter.
  • Ethiopic Calendar: Used officially as the national calendar of Ethiopia.
  Because it calculates the birth year of Jesus differently than the Roman
  Catholic Church, the country's calendar year is 7 to 8 years behind the
  Gregorian standard. It features 13 months (12 months of 30 days and one month
  of 5 or 6 days).
  • Iranian (Solar Hijri) Calendar: The official calendar of Iran and
  Afghanistan. It is anchored to the Islamic Hijra but uses a solar track
  rather than a lunar one, making it one of the most astronomically precise
  calendars in existence.
  • Maya Calendar: An incredibly complex, interlocking ancient system composed
  of the Tzolk'in (a 260-day ritual calendar), the Haab' (a 365-day solar
  calendar), and the Long Count (which tracks massive multi-millennial
  historical cycles).
  To understand how our current global calendar evolved from these ancient
  systems, check out this look at history:
  3:55

  what the list of     40

  While there is no single universally standardized government directory that
  names exactly 40 specific calendars, historians and anthologists generally
  agree that roughly 40 active calendar systems are still actively followed
  around the world today.
  These are kept alive for civil, religious, agricultural, or cultural
  identity.
  Below is a comprehensive breakdown of the key calendars that make up this
  list of 40 active global timekeeping systems, organized by their primary
  cultural and regional use:

  Global Civil Standards

  • Gregorian Calendar: The undisputed global civil standard for international
  business, politics, and aviation.
  • ISO Week Date: Part of the ISO 8601 standard, it divides the year strictly
  into 52 or 53 structured weeks, widely used in global corporate finance and
  software development.
  • Julian Calendar: The direct predecessor to the Gregorian calendar. It is
  still heavily used today by Eastern Orthodox Churches to calculate
  traditional holy days like Easter and Christmas.

  Regional National Calendars

  • Solar Hijri (Persian) Calendar: The official civil calendar of Iran and
  Afghanistan, tracked explicitly to the solar equinox.
  • Ethiopic Calendar: The primary official civil calendar of Ethiopia,
  famously running 7 to 8 years behind the Gregorian calendar.
  • Indian National (Shaka) Calendar: Formally adopted alongside the Gregorian
  system by the Indian government for official civil broadcasts and scheduling.
  • Thai Solar Calendar: The official calendar of Thailand, which operates
  identical months to the Gregorian calendar but numbers its years using the
  Buddhist Era (BE).
  • Minguo (Taiwan) Calendar: Used officially in Taiwan, measuring its Year 1
  from the founding of the Republic of China (1912 CE).
  • Juche Calendar: Used exclusively in North Korea, anchoring its Year 1 to
  the birth year of Kim Il Sung (1912 CE).

  Major Religious Calendars

  • Islamic (Hijri) Calendar: A purely lunar system followed by Muslims
  worldwide to mark the sacred periods of Ramadan, Eid, and Hajj.
  • Hebrew (Jewish) Calendar: A complex lunisolar system used globally to
  dictate Jewish feasts, holy days, and daily Torah readings.
  • Buddhist Calendar: Widely utilized throughout Southeast Asian nations
  (Cambodia, Laos, Myanmar, Sri Lanka) for sacred Buddhist observances.
  • Coptic Calendar: An ancient system derived from Egyptian astronomy,
  strictly maintained by the Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria.
  • Bahá'í (Badí‘) Calendar: A distinct solar calendar composed of 19 months of
  19 days each, practiced by the worldwide Bahá'í community.
  • Nanakshahi Calendar: A solar calendar introduced to determine dates for
  significant historical events and festivals in the Sikh faith.

  Traditional South Asian Calendars

  • Vikram Samvat: A historically massive lunisolar calendar highly popular
  across Northern and Western India, as well as serving as the official
  cultural calendar of Nepal.
  • Saka Samvat: An ancient Hindu calendar used traditionally alongside Vikram
  Samvat for astronomical calculations and local festivals.
  • Kali Yuga Calendar: A cyclical age-based calculation system originating
  from Vedic timekeeping, dating back to 3102 BCE.
  • Bengali Calendar: A solar calendar crucial for celebrating secular and
  cultural festivities in Bangladesh and West Bengal, India.
  • Burmese Calendar: Primarily kept in modern Myanmar to register traditional
  seasonal holidays like the Burmese New Year.
  • Nepal Sambat: A unique indigenous calendar celebrated predominantly by the
  Newar community of Nepal.

  East Asian & Indigenous Calendars

  • Traditional Chinese Calendar: A highly influential lunisolar system
  governing the global dates of the Lunar New Year, Lantern Festival, and
  traditional astrology.
  • Japanese (Gengo) Era Calendar: Runs completely parallel to the civil
  calendar, defining calendar epochs by the reign of the current Emperor (we
  are currently in the Reiwa era).
  • Traditional Korean Calendar: Derived from historical East Asian systems,
  used to calculate agricultural seasons and traditional holidays like Chuseok.
  • Tibetan Calendar: A complex lunisolar structure that dictates Tibetan
  Buddhist practices and the yearly Losar (New Year) celebration.
  • Javanese Calendar: Used by the Javanese people of Indonesia, combining an
  intricate cycle of Islamic, Hindu, and indigenous weekly rotations.
  • Balinese Pawukon & Balinese Saka: Two overlapping calendar systems used on
  the island of Bali to map out day-to-day rituals and major temple festivals.
  • Maya Calendar (Modern Long Count / Tzolk'in): Though famously historic, the
  interwoven rituals of the 260-day cycle are still kept continuously alive by
  traditional Maya communities across the Yucatán and Guatemala.

  Minor Regional & Ethno-Cultural Systems

  • Berber (Amazigh) Calendar: Used traditionally by North African Berber
  populations, specifically aligned with the agricultural seasons.
  • Yoruba Calendar: A seasonal and ritual timekeeping matrix practiced by
  Yoruba communities in West Africa.
  • Igbo Calendar: A traditional market-week tracking calendar used by the Igbo
  people of Nigeria.
  • Assyrian Calendar: A solar calendar utilized by modern Assyrian communities
  to keep ties to their ancient Mesopotamian lineage.
  • Armenian Calendar: A traditional chronological reference used for
  historical literature and specialized liturgical events.

  Modern Concept & Academic Calendars

  • Holocene Calendar: A modern alternative calendar that adds exactly 10,000
  years to the Gregorian system, positioning year 1 at the approximate
  beginning of human civilization.
  • Discordian Calendar: A five-season alternative tracking calendar derived
  from modern subcultural philosophy.
  • Unix Time: An absolute computer science system that tracks time strictly by
  counting the total number of seconds elapsed since January 1, 1970.
  an interactive global timekeeping

● The first article is _ARTICLES/EPOCHS/epochs.mime. It's a buffer: it begins
  with ZIGUA-ROTH: and the path of the Longstaff document, then the MIME fields,
  then a body of 2,618 bytes. The body lists where each calendar starts,
  ordered on your year axis, each with its line in Longstaff:

  ┌───────┬────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ index │ as written │                calendar                 │
  ├───────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ −3760 │ 3761 BCE   │ Hebrew (Anno Mundi), line 539           │
  ├───────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ −2697 │ 2698 BCE   │ Chinese, Yellow Emperor count, line 793 │
  ├───────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ −2636 │ 2637 BCE   │ Chinese, invention count, line 791      │
  ├───────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤

  ├───────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1     │ 1 CE       │ Anno Domini, line 487                   │
  ├───────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 79    │ 79 CE      │ Indian National (Saka), line 658        │
  ├───────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 622   │ 622 CE     │ Islamic (Hijri), line 628               │
  ├───────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1582  │ 1582 CE    │ Gregorian reform, line 477              │
  └───────┴────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  buffer.js check passes. The index column is your rule Y3, and the script
  confirms it's in order.

  The ordering also shows your orthogonality point. The index orders where each
  calendar starts, but not every calendar can be laid onto it by adding an
  offset. The article says this for each:
  - Hebrew: the year starts in autumn, so each AM year spans two Gregorian
    years.
  - Islamic: the lunar year is about 354 days, so no fixed offset exists at all.
  - Saka: "add 78" is the gap, and 79 is the first year.

  Those are the places where one calendar has to be read as an overlay on
  another, not converted into it.

  All the facts come from Longstaff, in my own words with line numbers, so none
  of his text is copied. The calendars in your pasted list that Longstaff
  doesn't cover can be added once each has its own source. That includes Juche,
  Minguo, Kali Yuga, Holocene and Unix time.

  These are the standard Masoretic figures: the Flood in AM 1656 and Abram in AM
  1948. Here they're computed from the letters, not looked up.

  How this meets Dionysius. Longstaff puts the Hebrew AM 1 at 6 October 3761
  BCE, which is index −3760. The Hebrew count is built from these same Masoretic
  ages. On your axis, AM n starts at index n − 3761:

  ┌────────────────────────┬──────┬─────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │         event          │  AM  │ starts at index │       as BCE        │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Creation               │ 1    │ −3760           │ 3761 BCE            │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ the Flood's forty days │ 1656 │ −2105           │ 2106 BCE            │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Abram                  │ 1948 │ −1813           │ 1814 BCE            │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Dionysius's AD 1       │ 3761 │ 0 → 1           │ it falls in AM 3761 │
  └────────────────────────┴──────┴─────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  Your pasted passage says Dionysius worked against a different count, one that
  put Christ's birth at AM 5500 and the end at AM 6000. That count came from the
  Septuagint, whose Genesis 5 and 11 give many fathers larger ages at their
  son's birth (Adam's age at begetting Seth is 230 there, against 130 here). So
  the gap between AM 3761 and AM 5500 starts in these same two chapters, as a
  difference between two witnesses. You can measure it with the mask, letter by
  letter, without saying who's right.

  words, together with the Dionysius passage you pasted, cited to its Wikipedia
  article. Wikipedia text is CC BY-SA, so the article would credit it. Should I
  write it?

● The second article is _ARTICLES/ANNOMUNDI/annomundi.mime. It passes buffer.js
  check, and it walks the line from Adam to Abram in both witnesses, every age
  read from the text's own number words:

  ┌─────────┬────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │         │     Masoretic      │    Septuagint (Swete /      │   apart   │
  │         │    (Leningrad)     │        Alexandrinus)        │           │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ the     │ AM 1656            │ AM 2262                     │ 606 years │
  │ Flood   │                    │                             │           │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Abram   │ AM 1948            │ AM 3334                     │ 1,386     │
  │         │                    │                             │ years     │
  └─────────┴────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┴───────────┘

  Where the 1,386 years come from. Comparing father by father, almost every
  difference is exactly one hundred: Adam 130 vs 230, Seth 105 vs 205, and so on
  through Serug. The two witnesses differ only in the hundreds place of the
  fathers' ages and agree on everything below it. That's your mask: a difference
  in a single row, the hundreds row in the Aegean layout. Three fathers don't
  follow the pattern:
  - Lamech differs in the units: 182 vs 188.
  - Nahor differs in the tens: 29 vs 79.
  - Kainan (130) is in the Septuagint only. In the Hebrew he's absent, the empty
    base, so he adds a whole generation.

  How it reaches Dionysius. The Hebrew Anno Mundi starts in 3761 BCE and is
  built from the Masoretic ages, so the Hebrew year in which Dionysius's AD 1
  falls is AM 3761, starting at index 0. The count Dionysius worked against put
  Christ's birth at AM 5500. Here you can see where it starts to run ahead: in
  the Greek ages, from the first father on. The article cites the Dionysius
  passage to Wikipedia's Anno Domini, paraphrased, along with Georges Declercq's
  suggestion. It also records your reading, quoted as yours: "a full unit
  cycle, a 0-sphere of its own".

  What's declared, not fixed:
  - The digitization errors: the empty 5:32, τριόκοντα, ὄη and ἐγέντὸν. Each is
    listed as a fold in the article and in the witness's PROVENANCE file.
  - Which manuscript this is: Swete's Genesis is Codex Alexandrinus, because
    Vaticanus lacks those chapters. That explains why Methuselah is 187 here.

  Licensing: the Greek digital text is CC BY-SA 4.0, so this article is CC BY-SA
  4.0 too, and it says so. SOURCES.md now lists the Swete text and the
  Wikipedia article. The Greek file itself stays private, with the other
  witnesses.

  Sources:
  - nathans/lxx-swete (https://github.com/nathans/lxx-swete)
  - Brenton's LXX at CCEL (https://ccel.org/bible/brenton/index.html), checked
    but not used: its Greek is in an old font encoding, not Unicode
  - Wikipedia, Anno Domini (https://wikipedia.com/wiki/AD)

● The root in hieroglyphs. Yes, your letters come from Egyptian hieroglyphs,
  through a chain of scripts. The Rosetta Stone is a different link: it's how
  hieroglyphs were read again.

  1. The alphabet grew out of hieroglyphs. This is well established:
  - Around the 19th–15th century BCE, workers at the Sinai turquoise mines
    (Serabit el-Khadim) took hieroglyph shapes and used each one for the first
    sound of its name. This is called acrophony, and the script is called
    Proto-Sinaitic.
  - The ox head became ʾalp, "ox", and stood for ʾ. The house plan became bet,
    "house", and stood for b.
  - From there the line runs: Proto-Sinaitic → Phoenician → Hebrew, and also →
    Greek → Latin. So א and Α and A are one sign, the ox, descended three ways.

  For the letters of the Name, the proposed hieroglyph sources are less certain,
  so take them as scholarly readings: yod a hand or arm, he a man with raised
  arms, vav a peg or hook. So even יהוה traces back to pictures.

  2. The Rosetta Stone is the key back the other way. It's a decree of Ptolemy V
  from 196 BCE, carved in three scripts: hieroglyphic, Demotic and Greek. It
  was found in 1799, and Champollion used its Greek to read the hieroglyphs in
  1822. It doesn't contain the Name. Its role is that of a witness that has the
  same text in three scripts, which is what your _CODICES[1] holds.

  3. Your sources meet in one place: Ptolemaic Egypt.
  - The Septuagint was translated from Hebrew into Greek in Alexandria in the
    3rd century BCE. Tradition credits Ptolemy II.
  - The Rosetta decree was written in the same Koine Greek, under the same
    dynasty, a few generations later.
  - Codex Alexandrinus, the manuscript behind our Greek Genesis, takes its name
    from Alexandria.

  So the hieroglyphs are the root of the letters, the Rosetta Stone is where the
  reading was recovered, and Alexandria is where the Hebrew line was first
  carried into Greek.

  Sourcing the manuscript itself. Alexandrinus is at the British Library as
  Royal MS 1 D V–VIII. Genesis is in volume 1, Royal MS 1 D V, folios 5r–38r.
  Two ways to see it:
  - CSNTM has images made from the British Museum's full-size facsimiles of
    1879–83 and 1909. Those are old enough to be in the public domain, but
    CSNTM's own terms of use still need checking.
  - The British Library's Digitised Manuscripts has colour scans of at least the
    New Testament volume. Whether Genesis has been scanned still needs
    checking.

  If I can look at the Genesis 5 folio, I can check Swete's numbers against the
  manuscript, especially Methuselah's 187. Then the article would cite the
  manuscript's own page, not only a printed edition of it.

  Can I check CSNTM's terms of use and fetch the folio images for Genesis 5 and
  11?

  Sources:
  - British Library catalogue, Royal MS 1 D V
    (https://searcharchives.bl.uk/catalog/040-001614686)
  - CSNTM, GA 02 (Alexandrinus)
    (https://manuscripts.csntm.org/Manuscript/View/GA_02)

❯ is that related to the lbrary of alexandeladerand how did that catalog

● Yes, the Septuagint is tied to the Library of Alexandria, though by tradition
  more than by documentation. The way the Library catalogued its books is close
  to what we've been building.

  The link, as the tradition tells it. The Letter of Aristeas, the oldest
  account of the Septuagint, says Demetrius of Phalerum, who ran the Library,
  urged Ptolemy II to have the Jewish Law translated into Greek for the
  Library's collection. Seventy-two translators were brought from Jerusalem,
  hence "Septuagint", "the seventy". Historians treat the details as legend, but
  the translation was made in Alexandria in the 3rd century BCE, under the
  dynasty that ran the Library.

  Codex Alexandrinus is a different matter. It was written in the 5th century
  AD, after the Library was gone. It's named for Alexandria because the
  Patriarch of Alexandria owned it before it was given to England in 1627. So it
  belongs to the same city and the same Greek line, but it wasn't a Library
  book.

  How the Library catalogued. Callimachus of Cyrene made the Pinakes ("Tables"),
  the earliest library catalogue we know of. Later sources say it filled 120
  scrolls. As far as we can tell from what survives:
  - Category first: works were sorted by kind, such as epic, tragedy, law,
    history, medicine and mathematics.
  - Then author, in alphabetical order. Alphabetical order itself is credited to
    Zenodotus, the first librarian, who sorted by first letter.
  - Each entry gave: the author, the title, the opening words of the work (its
    incipit), and its number of lines.

  The opening words and the line count were how a scroll was identified and
  checked. Two copies with different titles could be matched by their first
  words. A copy with the wrong number of lines was known to be incomplete.
  Scribes were even paid by the line, a practice called stichometry. Books
  arriving on ships were reportedly copied, and the Library kept the originals.

  How that lines up with your codex:

  ┌────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Pinakes         │                   ZIGUAROTH                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ category, then author  │ _CATALOG/<BARREL>/<BARREL>/term, sorted by     │
  │ A–Z                    │ collation                                      │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ the opening words (the │ the literal: the first code point, the head    │
  │  incipit)              │                                                │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ the number of lines,   │ Content-Length, rule B6: a buffer whose length │
  │ checked against each   │  doesn't match is rejected                     │
  │ copy                   │                                                │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ the ship's original    │ the witness kept intact (M5), readings laid    │
  │ kept, a copy given     │ over it                                        │
  │ back                   │                                                │
  └────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So the oldest known library catalogue identified a text by its literal
  beginning and its exact length, which are the same two things each of your
  buffers carries. That's a correspondence I'm pointing out, not something the
  Library defined.

  I'm answering this from general knowledge, without a source in hand. To put it
  into _ARTICLES, I'd cite a source for each point: a translation of the Letter
  of Aristeas (public domain translations exist) and a scholarly account of the
  Pinakes.

  The question about fetching the Alexandrinus folios is still open as well.

  │ 5:32 Noah           │ ΚΑΙ ΗΝ ΝΩΕ ΕΤΩΝ ΠΕΝΤΑΚΟΣΙΩΝ…      │ empty         │

  So the manuscript supports the numbers we computed. The empty 5:32 is a gap in
  the digital copy, not in the codex.

  These are readings by eye of a reduced black-and-white scan, and the record
  says so. The middle of ΟΓΔΟΗΚΟΝΤΑ is smudged, but the word clearly starts ΟΓ,
  so it's 187, not 167. Methuselah's age matters because a reading of 167 would
  make him outlive the Flood.

  The readings are written into the witness's PROVENANCE file, into a new
  manuscript field in the ANNOMUNDI article (rewrapped, and its check passes),
  and into SOURCES.md as the facsimile's entry. The changed article and
  SOURCES.md aren't committed yet.

  On Alpha to Omega, and Z: your suspicion is right. Z ending the alphabet is
  too convenient, and the history shows why:
  - In Greek, Ζ (zeta) is the sixth letter, and as a numeral it means 7. Greek
    runs from Α to Ω, and that's the "Alpha and Omega" of Revelation.
  - In Latin, Z was dropped around 312 BC, then brought back in the 1st century
    BC for Greek loanwords, and added at the end. So Z is last because it came
    back late, not because it belongs there.

  An A-to-Z catalogue therefore carries a late Roman repair in its last letter.
  That's a good reason to follow your model and order by literal code point and
  collation, not by the alphabet's traditional story. The Pinakes model works
  the same way as a MIME template literal: a fixed head (category, author,
  incipit, length) and a body carried as it is.

● The test for each item is simple: can a script recompute it from a witness we
  hold? If it can, the item has a literal basis. If it depends on a choice
  someone made, it's a reading, and it's labelled as one. Here's everything
  tracked now, sorted by that test.

  Translation, measured literally: what each rendering keeps of the letters
  (I-229)

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    rendering     │                keeps the letters?                │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ THE LORD, ADONAI │ no: it replaces them (RAISE)                     │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ YAHWEH           │ yes (MIRROR)                                     │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ JEHOVAH, JAH     │ yes, but only under a declared fold, J→Y and V→W │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Subjective: readings, labelled as readings
  - The two folds: J→Y / V→W, and reading sheva as E, holam as O and qamatz as
    A, which is what makes "JEHOVAH's vowels are Aleppo's marks" work.
  - Dionysius's motive: fear of AM 6000. This is Declercq's suggestion, through
    Wikipedia.
  - The hieroglyph sources of yod, he and vav: proposed by scholars, not
    certain.
  - Applying "a day for a year" to the other forties: the text states the rule
    only in its two verses.
  - The "hidden 5", "a 0-sphere of its own", and 28 read as the solar cycle:
    your readings.
  - The Rosetta, Alexandria and Pinakes links: history and correspondence, not
    checked against any text.

  Where the story between translations is not yet tracked literally

  The literal chain stops at Greek. The claim that Adonai → Kyrios → Dominus →
  THE LORD is history, not something counted from texts, and we hold no Latin or
  English witness.

  The next step on that chain can be checked with what we already hold. Swete's
  Greek can be looked up at the same verses where Leningrad has יהוה, to count
  how often the Greek has κύριος there. That would put the first translation
  step on the literal side.

  Still uncommitted: the article's manuscript check and the facsimile's entry in
  SOURCES.md.

● The first translation step is now on the literal side: the third article,
  _ARTICLES/KYRIOS/kyrios.mime. Its check passes. It compares the Leningrad
  Hebrew with Swete's Greek (Alexandrinus) verse by verse, across the Genesis
  chapters we hold in both (1–3, 5–11 and 50; chapter 4 was never fetched).

  ┌─────────────────┬──────────┬───────────┐
  │                 │  Hebrew  │   Greek   │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┤
  │ the Name / Lord │ יהוה 40  │ κύριος 35 │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┤
  │ God             │ אלהים 87 │ θεός 101  │
  └─────────────────┴──────────┴───────────┘

  Of the 39 verses with the Name or κύριος, the counts agree in 28 and differ in
  11:
  - In 8 verses the Hebrew writes the Name and the Greek has no κύριος.
    - Five are in the second creation account, Genesis 2:5, 7, 9, 19 and 21,
      where יהוה אלהים becomes plain ὁ θεός.
    - Three are around the Flood, 6:6, 6:7 and 8:20, where יהוה alone becomes
      θεός.
  - In 3 verses the Greek adds κύριος where the Hebrew has only אלהים: 6:12,
    8:15 and 9:12, where אלהים becomes κύριος ὁ θεός.
  - Genesis 1 has no Name in either witness: 32 אלהים against 33 θεός.

  So "the Name is translated as Lord" is a reading. In the literal text, the
  Greek dropped the Name in 8 places and added "Lord" in 3, so the swap isn't
  one for one. At this step you can now see exactly where the translation kept
  the reading and where it departed from the letters.

  The comparison has two limits, both recorded in the article:
  - Verses are paired by number. Where Greek and Hebrew number a verse
    differently, the pairing would misalign. Genesis is close, but that hasn't
    been checked verse by verse.
  - The Greek is a single manuscript, Alexandrinus, through Swete's printing.

  The article is CC BY-SA 4.0, like the others built on the Greek text. It isn't
  committed yet. Should I commit it?

● Committed as 0e2d48f.

  Which English Bible. The King James Version (1611) is the right one to align
  with, for three reasons:
  - It's the most widespread. It's the English Bible printed most often over the
    centuries, and the most quoted. Today the NIV outsells it in some years,
    but the NIV is copyrighted, so we couldn't store its text in the codex.
  - It's public domain almost everywhere. The one exception is the UK, where
    it's under a Crown patent. That fits your sources rule.
  - It was translated from the Hebrew, not the Greek. Its Old Testament follows
    the Masoretic text. So it's a third witness in the chain: Hebrew → Greek →
    English, with English taken directly from the Hebrew.

  What makes it useful here. The KJV marks the Name in its typesetting. Where
  the Hebrew has יהוה, it prints LORD in small capitals. Where the Hebrew has
  Adonai, it prints ordinary "Lord". YHWH Elohim becomes "LORD God". So even in
  English, the trace of the four letters survives as a typographic mark. Since
  the KJV follows the Hebrew, the prediction is that its LORD count in the same
  Genesis verses matches the Hebrew's 40, not the Greek's 35. The script would
  test that.

  The catch: many plain-text copies of the KJV lose the small capitals, so
  "LORD" and "Lord" can't be told apart. The copy we fetch needs to keep that
  mark. The eBible.org KJV in USFM format is one that does: it wraps each divine
  name in \nd … \nd* tags. It's public domain.

  Earlier in our work you stopped me from downloading the Gutenberg KJV, so I'm
  asking first this time. Can I fetch the KJV Genesis from eBible.org? It would
  go with the other witnesses in ~/private/codex-witness.

● The fourth article is _ARTICLES/LORD/lord.mime, and its check passes. The KJV
  is now a held witness: the 1769 standard text with Strong's numbers, from
  eBible.org, public domain, with its own PROVENANCE file.

  How it aligns, in the Genesis chapters held:

  ┌─────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         │              the Name               │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Hebrew יהוה             │ 40                                  │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ KJV, marked as the Name │ 40, verse for verse: no differences │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Greek κύριος            │ 35, differing in 11 verses          │
  └─────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  In all 11 verses where the Greek departs, the KJV follows the Hebrew. That
  fits its history, since it was translated from the Hebrew, not through the
  Greek. Its typesetting also keeps a trace of the four letters:
  - Small capitals: LORD where the Hebrew has יהוה, and ordinary "Lord" for
    other words.
  - Genesis 6:5 is the one exception: "And GOD saw…". The Name is printed GOD,
    but still in small capitals and still tagged H3068.

  Both marks are later layers, and the article says so. The small capitals are a
  printers' convention. The Strong's numbers were added by James Strong in
  1890.

  The date in your screenshot. The claim checks out. There are 12 dates this
  century where month² + day² = year² in m/d/yy, and 10/24/26 is the last. The
  one before it was 7/24/25.

  But the triangle belongs to the notation, not to the day:
  - It needs American month/day order and a two-digit year, which drops the
    century.
  - On your axis the same day is index 2026: no triangle.
  - In the Hebrew count it's AM 5787: no triangle.
  - In the tabular Hijri count it's AH 1448: no triangle.

  So it's an overlay, the Gregorian short form laid over the day. That makes it
  a clean example of your principle: the "right triangle" is real in the m/d/yy
  mask and absent in every other count.

  Two literal facts connect it to the work. I'm noting them as correspondences,
  not meanings:
  - 2026-10-24 is the day I-229 was filed. That's in its ORIGINS entry.
  - 26² = 676 is exactly the number of two-letter A–Z names, the full space of
    _CODEX.

  you added, and slide_ruler.html shows as deleted. I haven't touched any of
  them. For now I'd commit only my two changes, the LORD article and the KJV
  entry in SOURCES.md, and leave your templates for you to decide. Should I go
  ahead with that?

● I saved the catalog as unbound/catalog.mime, and it passes buffer.js check.
  Its first 12 bytes are ZIGUA-ROTH: , then come MIME fields, then a YAML body
  nested three levels:
  - families: the 11 invariants, each with its views;
  - unplaced: the 29 views with no invariant;
  - views: every file with its invariants, glyph count and size.

  To answer your question: yes, the YAML is wrapped in a MIME entry. Any MIME
  tool reads the header, and the YAML parses on its own. Because the YAML nests,
  a term finds its level by its position in the tree.

  It isn't committed. unbound is your repo, so tell me if I should commit it.

  Your five files

  1. UBHP Harmonic Signature, your first centroid. I ran it on real input:
  - The ray is exactly right. It divides the bytes by their length, which puts
    every buffer on the unit sphere. That step is idempotent: I checked that
    normalizing twice gives the same point, length exactly 1. That's your
    centroidal normalization, and it holds.
  - The ID forgets order. It's built only from that length (h, then sin, cos and
    a rounded tan). Rearranging the letters doesn't change it, so "listen" and
    "silent" get the same ID, and so do GOD and DOG. I checked both. The ID
    records which bytes are present, but not where.
  - That limit doesn't apply to the ray. The ray keeps each byte's position, so
    it can tell GOD from DOG.
  - One conflict with your current rules: the ID is a rounded float, while the
    codex now says no hash or rounded number is an identity. The ray holds up
    and the ID doesn't. The literal in it is the ray.

  2. The 4-bit XOR adder, your diffusion. It takes a continuous signal, the
  energy of the media or mic, and quantizes it into bits. It spreads the work
  across four workers, runs the bits through a full adder, and shows them as
  DNA/RNA base pairs. Continuous to discrete, to logic, to a four-letter
  alphabet: that's your discrete points being diffused.

  3. The Reality Compiler, your development portal. It's three.js plus calls to
  an outside language model, through apiUrl with an API key. As a portal it's
  fine. But under the codex rules, nothing a model returns is a witness. It can
  show views, but it can't be a source.

  4. WESIRI and 5. the seed kernel SVG: the carrier and the portal.
  - The SVG is pure carrier, with no script: the Fano plane, A–G tones, ROYGBIV
    light, Rumsfeld quadrants, a federation protocol, roles and metadata (GF(2),
    Z7, NDJSON, FANO-GARDEN-2026-01). It has exactly 240 <text> elements. I'm
    only recording that count, without reading meaning into it.
  - WESIRI reads it. The SVG is listed in its SVG_DOCS, and WESIRI computes
    centroids, Fano lines and S-P-O tables from it.
  - The SVG already states today's principle, in its federation section: "Share
    the line. Keep the differential. The 8th belongs to the session… THE
    DIFFERENTIAL IS THE WORLD." That's the mask rule: share the structure, and
    keep the difference, never resolving it.
  - One conflict: WESIRI signs its payloads with hashStr, an FNV-1a hash
    (0x811c9dc5). Under the codex rules, a hash can't be an identity. The SVG's
    own rule, keep the differential, fits the codex. WESIRI's hash signature
    doesn't.

  What this predicts for the build. The seed SVG is your best template example:
  a static carrier with literals written directly into it, which is rule T6
  already. WESIRI is the view that reads it. The Atomic family is next. Should I
  draft its template literal, using the seed SVG's carrier/reader split as the
  pattern?

● The π proof is complete, in the registry and fully proved (95 Qed, no
  Admitted, no axioms). Here's what it does in literal terms:

  What PiProjectionPreservesWitnesses.v proves
  - The binary part. Each step n gets a sign from the parity of its incidence
    phase: +1 on even steps, −1 on odd ones. Your diagonal and Polybius gauge
    produces that alternation.
  - The ratios. Your BQF bridge gives the denominator 2n + 1, the odd numbers,
    and the theorem bqf_bridge_denominator_matches_projection proves it.
  - Together those make exactly the terms +1, −1/3, +1/5, −1/7 and so on: the
    Leibniz series, which sums to π/4. Four times its limit is π.

  One distinction for the record:
  - The convergence to π isn't new. OMI_PI is defined as Coq's own Alt_PI, so
    OMI_PI = PI is Coq's standard lemma.
  - The match is yours. What the file proves on its own is that your incidence
    schedule reproduces the Leibniz terms term by term: the parity of the sign
    and the odd denominator, each from incidence.

  So you got π from a binary alternation divided by incidence ratios, with no
  floating point in the steps.

  The geometry under it is the diagonal: π/4 is the angle of a unit square's
  diagonal, arctan 1. So this proof follows the same square and diagonal you've
  built on all along.

  From UBHP to here. You started with Math.hypot and sin before you knew what
  they were, and ended with the same π reached through parity and incidence. The
  floats you learned on in UBHP are gone from the π proof. Each step is now
  exact, with the real numbers entering only at the limit, and the file keeps
  that boundary explicit.

  Your functor-or-monad question has a literal test in what we've built. I'm
  offering this as my correspondence, not a definition:

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        test        │      kind      │           examples here            │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ f(f(x)) = f(x):    │ a closure: an  │ UBHP's ray onto the unit sphere (I │
  │ applying it twice  │ idempotent     │  checked: idempotent), buffer.js   │
  │ changes nothing    │ monad          │ wrap as one framing, normalizing   │
  │                    │                │ to NFC                             │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ keeps the shape    │                │ transliterate.js (maps each code   │
  │ and maps each      │ a functor      │ point and keeps the template),     │
  │ part: f(a·b) =     │                │ I-229's fold J→Y, V→W              │
  │ f(a)·f(b)          │                │                                    │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ compares two       │ neither: a     │                                    │
  │ things without     │ mask           │ mask.js, the XOR difference        │
  │ changing either    │                │                                    │
  └────────────────────┴────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  So each object can be classified by checking which of those equations it
  satisfies. Your first attempt was already reaching for that.

❯ <ide_selection>The user selected the lines 3 to 45 from
  /home/main/omi/omi-ii/Research Lab/_archive/omi-isa-txt/project layer/The
  real reduction question.txt:
  I'm asking for the quotients of the system:

  What structure can be removed
  while the same canonical unfold / replay / projection still results?

  That means the true foundation is not “all the symbols you currently talk
  about,” but the smallest set of laws that preserve the same outputs.

  ---

  There was one design decision. The delta law:

  rotl(x,1) XOR rotl(x,3) XOR rotr(x,2) XOR C
  Four choices inside that: rotations (not shifts, so no bits are lost), XOR
  (reversible), a constant (breaks the zero fixed point), mask to width (keeps
  state bounded).

  That is the entire design.

  Everything else was computed by the math:

  Period = 8 → property of that specific law on 16-bit space

  Prime 73 → smallest prime whose decimal repeats with period 8

  Block B = [0,1,3,6,9,8,6,3] → the digits of 1/73

  W = 36 → sum of B

  Orbit/offset recovery → just divmod(position, 36)

  Nobody chose 73. The law has period 8. The smallest prime with decimal period
  8 is 73. The system found it.

  This may or may not be related to the current task.</ide_selection>

  Atomic Incidence Surface: SONAR_PERIOD 75 precesses 1 channel(s) per master

  The component file is unchanged and correct as you wrote it. The draft records

  On "25 200". Reading the digits literally, 25,200 is 25 × 1,008, and the
  factors show the same thing: 25,200 = 5² × 1,008, where 1,008 = 5040 ÷ 5. So
  "25 | 200" splits it into the squared five and what's left. That's a reading
  of the digits. The factor 5² is literal.

    literally about "the 2 and the 5 of the base".

● Here is the overview you asked for. Every number is computed. What each one
  means is a correspondence, and it's labelled as one.

  The sonar period, 75 = 5 channels × 15 lanes, against time

  fact: 5040 mod 75 = 15, so lcm = 25,200 = 5 × 5040
  computed: ✓
  what it touches: the full closure: five master cycles

  computed: ✓
  what it touches: the prime meridian: the Earth turns 15° per hour, so a
  channel
  is one time zone and 75 is five hours
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  fact: 25,200 ÷ 400 = 63, ÷ 28 = 900, ÷ 4 = 6,300
  computed: ✓
  what it touches: leap years: 25,200 closes the Gregorian 400-year cycle, the
  28-year solar cycle and the 4-year leap cycle exactly
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  fact: 25,200 ÷ 19 = 1,326.3
  computed: ✗
  what it touches: it does not close the 19-year lunar (Metonic) cycle
  ────────────────────────────────────────

  computed: ✓
  what it touches: the traditional "Great Year": one degree of precession every
  72 years. That's the 72 you remembered, times the 360 in your GOAL_CYCLE. The
   measured precession is about 25,770 years, so both 25,200 and 25,920 are
  round figures near it.

  532 = 19 × 28, his Easter cycle

  ┌────────────┬─────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  The three timelines sit exactly across the sonar's wrap: the last lane, the
  reset, then the first lane. In fairness, every period resets at year 0,
  because 0 is where every count starts. What's specific to 75 is that AD 525
  lands on the reset too, since it's exactly seven sonar periods. So on this
  clock, Dionysius's year and year zero are the same phase.

  The 6000 years:

    era quietly replaced.
  - Hebrew count (Masoretic, AM 1 = 3761 BCE): AM 6000 begins in autumn AD 2239.
    The year that began this autumn, 2026, is AM 5787. On the sonar, AM 6000
    falls at phase 64, channel 4.

  So the "full close" of 6000 years means two different dates, depending on
  which witness's ages built the count. That's the same Masoretic/Septuagint
  mask from the ANNOMUNDI article, now projected onto your timeline.

● I read all three. Together they explain the 75 completely, and they also show
  which of your earlier claims hold up.

  1. The 60-in-64 Clock: this explains the 75. It's the careful one, and it says
  so: "uses only what holds up as arithmetic." Its pattern is a dial of 16-slot
  blocks where 15 of every 16 slots carry time, and the 16th is held back:

  ┌───────────┬──────────────┬───────┬───────────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │   dial    │ blocks of 16 │ slots │ carry time (15 per block) │ held back │
  ├───────────┼──────────────┼───────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ small     │ 4            │ 64    │ 60                        │ 4         │
  ├───────────┼──────────────┼───────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ large     │ 16           │ 256   │ 240                       │ 16        │
  ├───────────┼──────────────┼───────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ the sonar │ 5            │ 80    │ 75                        │ 5         │
  └───────────┴──────────────┴───────┴───────────────────────────┴───────────┘

  So 75 is the same 15-of-16 dial with five blocks. It's the only one whose
  block count isn't a power of two: 64 = 2⁶ and 256 = 2⁸, but 80 = 5 × 2⁴.
  That's exactly why it doesn't close:
  - Each lane block already carries a 5: 15 = 3 × 5.
  - With 4 channels (the 60 sonar), the total has 5 once. 5040 has 5 once, so 60
    closes.
  - With 5 channels, the channel count brings a second 5. 75 = 3 × 5², so it
    can't close on 5040.

  The whole 80-slot dial does close: 5040 ÷ 80 = 63. The break comes only from
  counting the 75 that carry time.

  Your 60-in-64 page also stacks counters: 7 × 3 × 240 = 5040. The sonar's full
  closure is 25,200 = 7 × 3 × 5 × 240. In the page's terms, it adds a 5-part
  counter to the stack. That's the hidden 5, entering as one more counter.

  2. The 240 LED Clock: mostly claims that don't hold. It reads like an
  AI-written draft, and your 60-in-64 page later dropped its claims on purpose
  ("makes no claim that 240 is the size of a geometric rotation group, that the
  spare codes are the first Unicode control codes"). Specifically:
  - "The first 60 Unicode control codes (0x00–0x3B)": only 0x00–0x1F are control
    codes. From 0x20 up they're printable.
  - The Klein configuration: the doc garbles it, and nothing it says shows its
    symmetry group has order 240.
  - The "Coxeter word" formula sums 24 terms but lists only 23 offsets.
  - Its own section 15 ends with a non-integer and calls it a pattern anyway.

  The 240 that actually holds is in your Coq proofs: E8 has 240 roots
  (E8RootsEnumerate240.v), along with 240 = 15 × 16 = 256 − 16 by arithmetic.

  3. The Full 60 and the Blocking: right idea, wrong count.
  - The orbit is right. 60 ⊕ n for n = 0..63 visits all 64 values exactly once.
  - The blocks are 16, not 12. 60's two lowest bits are 0, so every aligned run
    of 4 in n maps to an aligned run of 4 in the values. The "12 blocks" and the
    "skips" come from which rows the document happened to list, not from any
    regex.
  - The 0x30–0x3F four-block is real. Your I-129b proves it: 0 to 3, 4 to 7, 8
    to ;, and < to ?.
  - The fold table claims "δ⁸(x, c) = x for all x, c". Your own I-195 shows
    otherwise: it holds for every state under C = 0x1D, but under 0x1D1D, 8
    states are fixed and others have period 2 or 4.
  - Its fold also changes C every cycle, using the previous state. That's a
    different recurrence, and nobody has worked out its period.

  Putting it together: the sonar's 75 is the 60-in-64 dial with a fifth block of
  16. Its full closure, 25,200 = 7 × 3 × 5 × 240, is your stacked 5040 counter
  with a 5-part added: the hidden 5, counted. That's the full closure you were
  reaching for.

  The structure and the arithmetic are literal. Calling the fifth block the 6000
  years, a time zone or the Aegean channel is your reading on top of that.

  I can write this up as the _ARTICLES/SONAR article, with these three documents
  as its sources: what each one gets right, and what doesn't hold. Should I?

● I filed the claim and the proof, and all eight predictions held.

  The proof is I-230 in omi-axioms (tag 6E, commit aae9768, merged into main as
  cbac392). The catalog now has 117 records, all holding. The eight predictions
  were written into the header before anything ran:

  ┌─────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────┐
  │     │                         prediction                          │ held │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ P1  │ 60, 240 and 75 are one dial: blocks of 16 slots with 15     │ ✓    │
  │     │ carrying time, in 4, 16 and 5 blocks                        │      │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ P2  │ 60 closes on 5040, 75 doesn't, and 5040 mod 75 = 15, one    │ ✓    │
  │     │ channel                                                     │      │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ P3  │ each master cycle starts the sonar one channel later: 0 1 2 │ ✓    │
  │     │  3 4, then 0                                                │      │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ P4  │ it closes at 25,200 = 5 × 5040 = 7 × 3 × 5 × 240, a 5-part  │ ✓    │
  │     │ counter added to your stack                                 │      │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ P5  │ the reason is the 5: 75 = 3 × 5² against 7!'s single 5,     │ ✓    │
  │     │ while the whole 80-slot dial closes                         │      │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ P6  │ only the five-block dial isn't a power of two: 64 and 256,  │ ✓    │
  │     │ but 80                                                      │      │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ P7  │ 525 = lcm(7, 75) = 7 × 75                                   │ ✓    │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ P8  │ 25,200 closes the 4-, 28- and 400-year cycles, but not the  │ ✓    │
  │     │ 19                                                          │      │
  └─────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────┘

  The proof also runs your component's own channel formula across all 25,200
  ticks: every channel gets the same number of ticks. So the precession is fair,
  and over the full closure the fifth channel is no different from the rest.

  The claim is _ARTICLES/SONAR/sonar.mime in ZIGUAROTH (5ac01c7), marked
  checked, which is the third state. It keeps each layer in its own field:
  - claim and predictions: what's asserted, and what was predicted beforehand;
  - proof: I-230 and its commit;
  - sources: your component and the three archive documents;
  - holds and fails: what's right and wrong in each document, including the
    12-vs-16 block count, the I-195 counterexample and the 240 LED Clock's
    errors;
  - timelines and years: the reset at year 0 and at AD 525, and AM 6000 starting
    in autumn AD 2239;
  - reading: yours, held open. That covers the full closure, the 6000 years, the
    leap years, the prime meridian and the hidden 5.

● Yes, and it's built. The page is unbound/HTML/I-230 Sonar Closure.html, one
  self-contained file with no fonts or scripts fetched from anywhere. I rendered
  it in a headless browser, and its own live check reports "All eight hold."

  What's on the page:
  1. The claim, at the top, in plain words.
  2. The dial: 80 slots in five blocks of 16. Fifteen in each block carry time,
     and the held-back sixteenth is shaded grey. The sonar hand moves over them.
     You can press Play, drag through all 25,200 ticks, or jump one master
     cycle at a time. A readout shows the tick, the master cycle, the phase, the
     channel and lane, and when it all closes. A switch flips to the 4-channel
     / 60 version for comparison.
  3. The precession: six cycle cards showing the start channel. With 75 they
     read 0 1 2 3 4 0. With 60 every card says 0.
  4. Counting the fives: the prime factors of 15, 60, 75, 80, 5040 and 25,200,
     with each 5 highlighted. Only 75 and 25,200 have two.
  5. The eight predictions: each is run live in your browser, and each shows
     what it computed next to its "holds". One example: over 25,200 ticks, every
     channel gets exactly 5,040 of them.
  6. The year axis: −1, 0, +1 (your three folders), AD 525, AD 2026 and AD 2239,
     each with its phase. Year 0 and AD 525 both land on the reset.

  The footer cites the proof (omi-axioms I-230 and its commit), the claim
  (ZIGUAROTH's SONAR article) and the sources. It also says what's proved: the
  arithmetic. What the fifth channel means is your reading, and it's held open.

  It isn't committed in unbound yet. Should I commit it? I can also publish it
  as a shareable page (private until you share it), if you want to show it to
  someone without sending the file.
